A2 is rejected on Mojo 1.1.0 because mutation through its stored `Pointer` is invisible to the compiler's interior-reference invalidation.
- A1 registrations are `mut self` calls on `App`. A reference into `App._routes` taken before one is rejected (`scoped_direct_get_invalidates_interior_reference.mojo`: `use of invalidated interior reference`).
- The same write through an A2 registrar that was live before the reference was taken compiles, and after the route list reallocates, the old reference reads freed memory. `tests/state_known_gaps/registrar_blind_mutation.mojo` retains this: `check.sh` builds it and never runs it. The registrar must exist first, because `with_state` is itself a `mut self` call that invalidates an earlier reference.
- A2 would therefore make Muntin's own public API a mutation path the borrow checker does not see. Its safety would rest on a project invariant ("`App`'s public API returns no reference into `App`") instead of on the compiler. With no private fields on 1.1.0, `_routes` is reachable by name even today.
- The registrar would also add a mutable origin-escape hazard: constructed with `MutAnyOrigin`, it can write to a freed `App`. `TestClient[ImmutAnyOrigin]` can already read from one; that existing read-side hole is no reason to add a write-side one.

A1's costs are all DX: longer diagnostics for failing M2 calls, and the state repeated per route. Diagnostics can be improved and sugar added later. A blind mutation path in the public architecture is hard to remove. A2 is a sound idea that is early for Mojo 1.1.0 (revisit conditions below).

**The static rule.**

- *Injected:* a registration with two arguments, `(handler, state: State[S])`. The handler's first parameter is `State[S]`, and `S` is inferred from both arguments, so they must agree (`state_fail/mismatched_state_type.mojo`: `value passed to 'state' cannot be converted from 'State[Cache]' to 'State[Db]'`). After it comes exactly one M2 shape, bound by M2-005 unchanged: route values in the literal's order, then the body.
- *Route value:* `Int` in a route-value position, as in M2. *Body:* the last parameter on `post`, `B: FromBody`, as in M2. *Raw:* `def(State[S], var Request) -> Response`, the M2-015 shape with the state first.
- *Separation from M2:* every M2 call passes one argument, and Mojo filters candidates by argument count before ranking. The stateful family never competes with an M2 overload. A stateful handler registered without its state matches no `get` overload (`get_state_without_argument.mojo`: the stateful candidates' note `missing required argument: 'state'`). On `post` it reaches the generic body overload with `B = State[...]` and fails there. A stateless handler given a state also fails (`state_for_stateless_handler.mojo`), so the state is never silently ignored.
- *Inside the family:* only `post` needs ranking. A stateful raw handler also satisfies the stateful `ToResponse` body overload (`B = Request`, `R = Response`). M2-014's shorter-list rule selects the raw overload: `[S, E, path]`, 3 parameters, against `[S, B, E, R, path]`, 5. The `String` body overload is not viable for a `Response` result. Mutation: two defaulted parameters on the raw overload make the lists equal, and the call becomes `ambiguous call to 'post'`. The two `get` overloads with one parameter after the state, `def(State[S], Int)` and `def(State[S], var Request)`, do not overlap.
- *Disjointness kept:* `State` is a Muntin type in a fixed position. It is not `Int`, it does not conform to `FromBody`, and the stateful body overloads reject a second `State` in the body slot by its private marker trait (`two_states.mojo`: `a handler takes at most one State, as its first parameter`). The application's `S` may be any type, `Int` included: the wrapper is what marks the parameter, not `S`. The placeholder-count checks are unchanged: the state is not a route value (`route_count_with_state.mojo` gets `def()`'s message).
- *Position:* state first is a design rule, not a compiler constraint. A state-last family (`def(Int, var B, State[S])`, DX section 8's old sketch) compiles and selects correctly in a scratch probe. It would make the body the second-to-last parameter on `post`, which restates M2-005's "the remaining last parameter is the body". State first leaves that sentence literal and gives one reading order: injected, route values, body. `state_last.mojo` and `state_after_body.mojo` pin that such handlers do not register.

**Shapes measured** (spike: `StateApp` = production's ten overloads with signatures and asserts copied, the four body overloads gaining the `State` guard, plus ten stateful ones declared before them, all stored in production `_Erased`):

| Method | Stateful shape | Results | Errors |
|---|---|---|---|
| `get` | `def(State[S])`, `def(State[S], Int)` | `String`-compatible, `R: ToResponse` | `thin raises E`, inferred, as M2-011 |
| `post` | `def(State[S], var B)`, `def(State[S], Int, var B)` | same | same |
| `get`, `post` | raw `def(State[S], var Request)` | `Response` | same |

Every request rule is M2's, decided in the same places: 404 without a match; 400 for an invalid route value, a missing, duplicated or invalid query value, or a `from_body` raise, all before the handler (`Users.calls` stays 0); `ToErrorResponse` or the fixed 500 for a raise (`NotFound` 404, bare `raises` 500 without its text); first registration wins. Raw stateful handlers receive the whole `Request` with no pre-handler 400.

**Overload cost: one factor of two, accepted.** The full family is 10 overloads, mirroring the 10 M2 ones: 5 shapes × 2 result policies, with the raw shapes `Response` only. This is the "one overload per permutation" red flag along exactly one axis. The judgment:

- The state axis is binary: with or without. `S` is generic, so more state types add no overloads. `E` is inferred, so errors add none.
- Mojo 1.1.0 offers two ways around the factor, and the compiler closes both. No variadic function types: `variadic_handler_type.mojo`, `expected a type, not a value`. No storable closures: `closure_handler_storage.mojo`, where a function generic over a closure trait is itself `capturing` and does not convert to `_Call[F]`.
- Rule that bounds it: a handler has **one injected slot, the first parameter, and it holds one `State`**. A later injected kind (request context, typed headers) either goes through `S` or `Request`, or needs its own decision. Any new kind that adds a slot doubles the overload count again.
- The factor also applies forward. Every later M2-style addition (`put`/`patch`/`delete`, non-`Int` route values, more arities) costs two overloads per result policy if it gets a stateful twin. Each such decision states whether it does.

**M2-005 rejected a registration argument before; why that does not carry over.** M2-005 candidate 2, a decoder passed at registration, was rejected for "a second argument on every body route and one decoder per position". M2-007 likewise rejected a converter at registration because it restated what the return type already says. The decoder and the converter restated something a type can carry statically, its conversion, and a trait carries it. State is a runtime value, and on Mojo 1.1.0 nothing else can carry it to a thin handler: no globals (`module_level_state.mojo`, `global variables are not supported`), no captures, and no generic `App` without infecting every backend (B1 below). The second argument appears only on the routes that take state, not on every route of a shape. The registration is the one place where the handler's type and the value meet statically. The repeated argument is the cost of that. A2 states it once per registrar, but at the safety cost above.

**Ownership and lifetime.**

- `State[S]` holds an `ArcPointer[S]` (std, reference counted with atomic counts; no unsafe operation). Superseded by M3-004 ("State storage decision (M3-004)"): through a second handle, `ArcPointer` lets code free memory a `state[]` reference points into, so production stores the value in a sealed box in the storage module; the public API below is unchanged. `State(value)` moves the value in. The application keeps its handle. `app.get[...](h, users)` borrows `users` and moves a copy of the handle into the route's `_Erased` box, together with the handler, as one private `_Bound[H, S]` value. The value lives until the last handle goes. Pinned in the spike: `Tracked` is dropped exactly once, after the application dropped its handle and then the moved `App`.
- Per request: the stateful adapter borrows the boxed `_Bound` (`_Erased.invoke` already borrows its value) and passes `bound.state` by borrow. No copy, no reference-count change, no allocation. In the spike, the count is the same inside a handler as between requests (4, for 3 routes and the application's handle). The mutation that copies the handle per request turns it red.
- `App` moves: the value is on the heap, so moving `App` (`var moved = app^`, twice in the spike) moves only the routes' handles. `TestClient(app)` borrows the `App` immutably, and `App.handle(self)` is read-only. Scratch copy: `TestClient` serves a stateful production `App` repeatedly, also after a move, while the application still reads its own handle.
- The compiler tracks every registration: each is a `mut self` call on `App`, so a reference into `App` taken before it is invalidated (`scoped_direct_get_invalidates_interior_reference.mojo`). No project invariant about references into `App` is needed (contrast A2).
- Read-only: `state[]` is `def __getitem__(self) -> ref[self._shared] S`. The receiver is a borrowed `self`, so the reference is immutable through every handle: borrowed, `mut` or owned (`mutate_through_state.mojo`, `expression must be mutable for in-place operator destination`). That line carries the guarantee; the mutation `ref self` makes the fixture compile. `ArcPointer[S][]` alone is mutable through a borrowed pointer (scratch probe), so `State` must not expose its pointer as API. Mojo 1.1.0 has no private fields, so `state._shared[].n += 1` compiles and mutates the shared value (review probe). The guarantee is "read-only through `state[]`", and `_shared` is an internal by name only. This is weaker than `_Erased`'s handle, which needs `unsafe_ptr()`. Read-only is shallow: a type `S` with interior mutability (the spike's `Users.calls`, its own `ArcPointer[Int]`) changes under its own rules, and Muntin does not synchronize it.
- Reference lifetime (M3-003 review): with the spike's `ArcPointer[S]`, a `ref` from `state[]` is not invalidated when its handle is reassigned, and then reads a dropped value. `ArcPointer.__getitem__` returns a reference with the handle's own origin (its source at the 1.1.0 tag has a FIXME saying this is not the right model), while `OwnedPointer[]` returns one the compiler tracks as interior. A first fix, `ArcPointer[OwnedPointer[S]]`, made reassignment a compile error but left a second handle able to replace the shared value. M3-004 ("State storage decision (M3-004)") decided the production representation: a sealed `_Shared[S]` that keeps the interior origin (`tests/state_get_fail/ref_after_*`) and closes the alias paths.
- Concurrency: today `App.handle` is never called concurrently. Flare's multi-worker `serve` requires a `Copyable` handler (each worker gets `H.copy()`), and `MuntinHandler` holds a move-only `App`. A backend that calls `handle` concurrently, or a copyable `App`, would make interior mutability in `S` a data-race question (revisit conditions).
- Several values: one `State` per handler. Several values are fields of one `S`. Different routes may bind different `S` (`State[Users]` and `State[Greeting]` in one app), and several routes may share one value (one count, both routes' calls visible through the application's handle). There is no registry, key or lookup.

**Effect on existing machinery.** `App` stays non-generic. `App.handle(Request) -> Response`, `_Route`, `_Erased`, `_Call[F] = def(F, List[String]) raises thin -> Response` and the unsafe surface are unchanged: `_Bound[H, S]` is one more `F`, and the stateful adapters are `_Call[_Bound[H, S]]`. `check_unsafe.sh` passes unchanged: `ArcPointer` is not an unsafe API. The storage module still names neither request data nor state. `Request`, `Response`, `TestClient`, the Flare adapter and the seam are unchanged.

**M2 stays closed.** No M2 public signature, binding rule or 400/404/500 boundary changes. Two scratch copies of the repository, not retained, each with the full stateful family on production: A1 (`src/muntin/state.mojo` with `State`, ten overloads, `_Bound` and five adapters in `app.mojo`, the `State` guard in the four body overloads) and A2 (the same with `with_state` and a registrar instead of the ten `App` overloads). In both, `./scripts/check.sh`, `./scripts/test.sh` and `./scripts/check_flare.sh` exit 0, with every existing suite count, fixture and expected text unchanged, plus one scratch `TestClient` test each. Diagnostics, compared in full for the 65 fixtures in `compile_fail`, `storage_fail` and `body_fail` that register on production `App` (normalized for paths and line numbers; `raw_fail` builds against the M2-014 spike, and five `storage_fail` fixtures never register):

| | identical | gain notes | of those, trailing detail notes omitted |
|---|---|---|---|
| A1, overloads on `App` (selected) | 49 | 16 (five `missing required argument: 'state'` each, after every M2 candidate's note) | 12 (Mojo's cap of ten notes per diagnostic; one to five notes, such as `result type of the first type is 'String' but the second type is 'R'`) |
| A2, registrar (rejected) | 65 | 0 | 0 |

Under A1, no candidate note, constraint message or error line is lost, and every expected text still matches. What gets shorter is the explanation a user reads for a failing M2 call. `scoped_app_keeps_detail_notes.mojo` pins A2's side of one such call. The `get` slice below adds four notes, to `get` calls only.

The body-overload guard, when it ships with the stateful `post` slice, changes only the message of calls that already fail, as M2-015's `Request` guard did. (Shipped in M3-006: "Stateful POST in production (M3-006)".)

**Name.** `State` collides with nothing in the Mojo 1.1.0 prelude (`use of unknown declaration 'State'`) or in Muntin. Flare 0.11 has its own `flare.http.State[T]`, a registration-time field of Flare's struct handlers that is copied per request and requires `Copyable & Defaultable`. Application code never imports Flare, and the adapter imports names explicitly, so the names do not clash. Muntin's type is independent of Flare's.

### State storage decision (M3-004)

Status: **decision** (M3-004; production is unchanged in it). It decides how `State[S]` stores the shared value. The public API decided in M3-001 is frozen: `State(value)`, `.copy()`, `state[]` read-only, a `State[S]` first handler parameter bound by `app.get[route](handler, state)`. Only the representation behind it changes.

**Why it was reopened.** M3-001 put an `ArcPointer[S]` in `State` and called it "safe std, no unsafe code". The PR #26 review of the M3-003 slice showed that this does not hold on Mojo 1.1.0:
- `ArcPointer.__getitem__` returns a mutable reference through any handle, borrowed ones included. Its source at the 1.1.0 tag has a FIXME saying the origin it returns is not the right model.
- Mojo 1.1.0 has no private fields, so `_shared` is reachable by name.
- Together: code holding a second copy frees memory a reference from `state[]` points into, through public std API alone and without writing `unsafe`. With `ArcPointer[S]`, `b._shared[].names.clear()` leaves `ref first = a[].names[0]` dangling (`tests/state_storage_known_gaps/arc_alias_clear.mojo`). With PR #26's `ArcPointer[OwnedPointer[S]]`, `b._shared[] = OwnedPointer(Db(2))` drops the value `ref r = a[]` reads (`arc_alias_swap.mojo`, the review's case).

This is the hazard class A2 was rejected for (M3-001): a stale reference the compiler does not see, reached through Muntin's names and public std API. It is not an M2 reopen: `App.handle`, `_Erased`, `_Call`, binding and 400/404/500 are untouched.

**The guarantee.** The review first asked that no name or safe pointer API reach the payload. Mojo 1.1.0 cannot meet that for any type, Muntin's or the stdlib's. The gate's fresh-context review showed it with primitives that need neither the word `unsafe` nor a stdlib-private field (`tests/toolchain_soundness_gaps`):
- **origin laundering:** `rebind[Pointer[T, MutAnyOrigin]](Pointer(to=x))` makes any borrowed value mutable. Through the public `state[]` alone, a second alias frees what a reference from the first points into, and the same works on a borrowed std `List`;
- **forged allocation:** public `alloc(...).into_thin()` makes an uninitialized `ThinAllocation`, and `swap` puts it in place of an owning field by name. It crashes `State`'s box and production `_Erased` on `main` alike;
- **`memmove`** (deprecated, a warning only): it bit-copies one handle over another;
- **stdlib-private fields** reached by name: `ThinAllocation._ptr`, `List._len`, `OwnedPointer._inner._ptr`, `app._routes._len` (M2-004, re-probed on `main`).

The guarantee M3-004 makes is therefore this: **Muntin must not introduce a Muntin-specific safe-code path that lets one `State` alias replace, mutate, swap, or prematurely destroy the shared payload observed through another alias. Toolchain-wide primitives that can violate the same property for standard-library types and existing Muntin storage (origin rebind, forged allocation or header replacement, `memmove`, stdlib-private fields) are outside this guarantee and are pinned as Mojo 1.1.0 toolchain-wide soundness gaps.** Candidates are compared on the ordinary path that is Muntin's own: an alias, then Muntin's representation, then the payload.

**Selected: A, a sealed shared box in the private storage module.**

```mojo
struct _SharedHeader[S: Movable & Deinitable](Movable):
    var count: Atomic[UInt64]
    var value: OwnedPointer[S]

struct _Shared[S: Movable & Deinitable](Copyable, Movable):
    var _header: ThinAllocation[_SharedHeader[S]]   # the only field
    def owned(self) -> ref[origin_of(self._header.unsafe_ptr()[].value)] OwnedPointer[S]

struct State[S: Movable & Deinitable](Copyable, Movable):   # public API unchanged
    var _shared: _Shared[S]
    def __getitem__(self) -> ref[origin_of(self._shared.owned()[])] S
```

- **Sharing:** the copy constructor increments the count; `__deinit__` decrements it, and the last handle drops the value and frees the header. The count is atomic with `ArcPointer`'s orderings (relaxed increment, release decrement, acquire fence before freeing), so copying a `State` across threads keeps `ArcPointer`'s guarantee.
- **Read-only:** `owned(self)` borrows its receiver, so every reference it yields is immutable, whatever handle it is reached through. That one line carries the guarantee: making it `ref self` lets a second handle assign the value (`alias_owned_assign.mojo` compiles). `State.__getitem__` adds nothing to it, so it no longer needs PR #26's `ImmOrigin(...)` wrapper (the mutation removing that wrapper stays green in A).
- **Lifetime:** the `OwnedPointer` inside the header gives the reference from `state[]` an origin interior to the handle (`a._shared._header.value["value"]`). Using it after the handle is reassigned, directly, through `mut`, or by assigning a new box to `_shared`, is `use of invalidated interior reference`. Using it after a move is `use of uninitialized value`, and returning it as a reference into the handle is `cannot return reference with incompatible origin`. With the value inline in the header instead, those fixtures compile (mutation), so the `OwnedPointer` is load-bearing.
- **Second handles:** pointing `b` at a new box (`b._shared = _Shared(...)` or `b = State(...)`) only drops `b`'s share; a reference from `a` keeps reading its value (`test_repointing_another_handle_keeps_the_value`). Code holding `b` cannot reach the shared value mutably through Muntin's names and public std API:
  - `b._shared[] = ...` is `'_Shared[Db]' is not subscriptable`;
  - `b._shared.owned() = ...` is `expression must be mutable in assignment`;
  - `b._shared.owned()[].names.clear()` is `invalid use of mutating method`;
  - `swap(b._shared.owned(), other)` is `value passed to mutable argument 'lhs' must be mutable`;
  - copying a header over another is `cannot be implicitly copied`, and moving one is `abandoned without being explicitly destroyed`.
- **Outside the guarantee (pinned):** each toolchain-wide primitive above still reaches the payload, `b._shared._header._ptr[].value = OwnedPointer(...)` and the forged-header swap included. Each is a must-build file in `tests/toolchain_soundness_gaps` (built without `--Werror`, because `memmove` warns). Two of them break production `_Erased` on `main` and a std `List` the same way, so none of them is specific to the box.
- **Per request:** unchanged from M3-001. A registration stores one copy of the handle with the handler in `_Erased`, and a request borrows it. In the spike, five calls through production `_Erased` leave the count at 3, and dropping the two boxes brings it back to 1.

**Cost.** Two allocations per `State` when it is built (header, `OwnedPointer`), as with PR #26's representation. Each access goes through two pointers. There is no request-path cost.

**Location and guard.** `_SharedHeader` and `_Shared` go into `src/muntin/_handler_storage.mojo`, which stays the only module allowed unsafe operations. Its docstring's scope widens from handler storage to Muntin's private storage. `scripts/check_unsafe.sh` changes in one rule: other modules may import `_Erased` or `_Shared` from it (`from ._handler_storage import _Erased` stays, and `state.mojo` adds `from ._handler_storage import _Shared`). `state.mojo` names no `OwnedPointer`, `ArcPointer` or unsafe API, so PR #26's one-word exemption for `state.mojo` is removed. Unsafe operations added to the storage module:
- `OwnedPointer.unsafe_take_allocation` + `Allocation.into_thin`: create the header;
- `ThinAllocation.unsafe_ptr`: read the header (count, accessor);
- `Pointer.unsafe_mut_cast` + `unsafe_origin_cast[MutUntrackedOrigin]` + `ThinAllocation(unsafe_owned_ptr=)`: the copy constructor's second owning handle on the same header;
- `ThinAllocation.unsafe_leak` + `OwnedPointer(unsafe_from_raw_pointer=)`: release the last handle.

The invariant the compiler does not check: every `ThinAllocation` of a header is counted exactly once, created only by `_Shared.__init__` (count 1) or its copy constructor (count + 1) and leaked only by `__deinit__` (count - 1), and the header and value are freed only at zero. `_Shared` exposes `owned` and `count` and nothing mutable.

**Candidates** (Mojo 1.1.0 (8189361e)):

| Candidate | Result | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| A. sealed `_Shared` (`ThinAllocation` header, atomic count, `OwnedPointer[S]`) in the storage module | public API unchanged; every ordinary alias path (replace, mutate, swap, assign through the accessor, header copy or move) rejected at compile time; reference lifetime and read-only compiler-checked; reachable only through the toolchain-wide primitives, as `_Erased` and std types are; two allocations per `State`; one guard rule widened | **chosen** |
| B. `ArcPointer[S]` (M3-001) | a second handle frees memory a `state[]` reference points into through an ordinary path, `_shared[]` (`arc_alias_clear.mojo`); reassigning the last handle leaves a `ref` dangling | rejected |
| B′. `ArcPointer[OwnedPointer[S]]` (PR #26) | reassignment checked, but a second handle replaces the shared `OwnedPointer` (`arc_alias_swap.mojo`, the review's case) | rejected |
| C. scoped access (`state.read[f]()`) or a handler-only opaque handle | not measured: both change M3-001's public API, which A keeps | not pursued |
| D. narrow the contract to "safe through public API" | contradicts the A2 and M2-004 bar | rejected |
| E. wait for the toolchain (`ArcPointer` indirect origins, private fields, a sound `rebind`/`alloc`) | the literal bar fails for every type on 1.1.0, `main`'s included, so waiting would block more than `State`; under the guarantee above, A meets it now | not needed |

**Evidence.**
- `tests/state_storage_spike.mojo` (library side: `_SharedHeader`, `_Shared`, `State`, and a `_Bound`-like box in production `_Erased`) + `tests/test_spike_state_storage.mojo` (5 tests): copies share one value and count; the value is dropped once after the last handle, also after a move; repointing a second handle keeps the first's value; reassigning releases one share; requests borrow the boxed handle.
- `tests/state_storage_lib_only/driver.mojo`: built by `check.sh` without the application module; boxes and shares a state type the library has never seen.
- `tests/state_storage_fail` (11): the alias cases above (subscript replacement, accessor assignment, value mutation, accessor swap), header copy and move, mutation through `state[]`, the reference after reassignment (direct, through `mut`, of the box) and returned from a handle.
- `tests/state_storage_known_gaps` (2, must build): B and B′ as above, each an ordinary alias path.
- `tests/toolchain_soundness_gaps` (5, must build, warnings allowed): `rebind_origin` (through `state[]` and a std `List`), `alloc_forge_state_box`, `alloc_forge_erased` (production `_Erased` on `main`), `memmove_handle`, `std_private_field`.
- Mutations (planted in the spike, reverted; 6), each red: `owned(ref self)` (`alias_owned_assign` and `alias_owned_swap` compile); a mutable subscript on `_Shared`; a copy without the increment (crash); a drop that frees on every handle (crash); the value inline in the header (the three reassignment fixtures compile); the handle copied per call.
- Scratch copy of the M3-003 branch with A applied (not retained): `_Shared` in the storage module, `State` over it, the exemption removed and the import rule widened, plus the two alias fixtures in `tests/state_get_fail`. `./scripts/check.sh`, `./scripts/test.sh` (`test_state` 11/11, every suite unchanged) and `./scripts/check_flare.sh` exit 0, with 14 `state_get_fail` fixtures passing.

**Revisit when:**
- a file in `tests/toolchain_soundness_gaps` stops building: the toolchain improved. Reevaluate whether the sealed box and its unsafe boundary can be simplified, and narrow the guarantee's exclusions;
- `arc_alias_swap.mojo` or `arc_alias_clear.mojo` stops building: `ArcPointer` no longer hands out mutable references through shared handles (its FIXME is resolved). Re-measure B, which needs no unsafe code;
- Mojo gets private fields: `_shared` and `_header` can be sealed by visibility, and the guard rule can narrow again.

**Next production slice (M3-003, PR #26, rebased).** Implemented in M3-003 as listed (`docs/SPEC.md`, "M3-003 result").
- `_handler_storage.mojo` gains `_SharedHeader` and `_Shared` as in the spike, its docstring's scope, unsafe list and downstream paragraph updated to the boundary above (ordinary paths closed; the forged-header replacement of `_Erased._header` named among the toolchain-wide gaps);
- `state.mojo` holds `_Shared[S]`, with `__getitem__(self) -> ref[origin_of(self._shared.owned()[])] S`, and imports `_Shared` instead of `ArcPointer`/`OwnedPointer`;
- `check_unsafe.sh` drops the `state.mojo` exemption and allows importing `_Shared`;
- `tests/state_get_fail` gains `alias_payload_swap` and `alias_value_mutation`, and keeps the `ref_after_*` fixtures;
- tests read the count through `_shared.count()`.

Everything else in PR #26 stands.

### Headers decision (M3-002)

Status: **decision** (M3-002; production and adapters are unchanged in it). It decides how Muntin-owned `Request` and `Response` carry HTTP header fields, how raw handlers reach them, what the Flare adapter maps, and what stays out of the first production slice.

**Selected: A, `muntin.Headers`, an ordered list of fields.**

```mojo
struct Headers(Copyable, Movable, Sized):
    def add(mut self, name: String, value: String) raises      # appends; earlier same-name fields stay
    def set(mut self, name: String, value: String) raises      # removes every same-name field, then appends
    def get(self, name: String) -> Optional[String]            # first value; None when absent ("" is a value)
    def get_all(self, name: String) -> List[String]            # every value, in order
    def __len__(self) -> Int
    def name(self, i: Int) -> String                          # as received or added
    def value(self, i: Int) -> String
```

- **Fields:** one list of name/value pairs. Each field keeps its position and its name's original casing. A repeated name is several fields, in order (`Set-Cookie`, `Vary`, `X-A` then `x-a`). Lookup compares names ASCII case-insensitively. There is no list joining and no reordering.
- **Validation at insertion:**
  - Names must be RFC 9110 tokens (non-empty).
  - Values may contain anything except control bytes (0x00–0x1F except HTAB, and 0x7F), and may not begin or end with SP or HTAB (RFC 9110 field-value; a receiver would trim them). Bytes 0x80 and up are allowed as the UTF-8 a Mojo `String` holds.
  - `add` and `set` raise otherwise, so no field added through the API can carry CR, LF or NUL.
  - The accepted set covers what pinned Flare's strict HTTP/1.1 parser accepts inbound (tokens; VCHAR, SP, HTAB, trimmed at both ends).
- **Ownership:** `Copyable` with an explicit `.copy()`, not implicitly copyable (`headers_fail/implicit_copy.mojo`), like `Request` and `State`.
  - `Request` gains `headers: Headers`, and its initializer gains a last, defaulted argument, `var headers: Headers = Headers()`, which moves it in: a caller passes `headers^` or `headers.copy()`, and a plain variable is a compile error (`headers_fail/request_headers_moved_in.mojo`). Every existing `Request(method, target[, body])` call is unchanged.
  - `Response` gains `headers: Headers`, empty from `Response(status, body)` and `Response.text`.
  - A raw handler that borrows its request cannot change the request's headers (`headers_fail/borrowed_request_headers.mojo`); `var req` owns a copy and can.
- **Bytes:** values are `String`, holding valid UTF-8. Over HTTP/1.1, Flare's default (strict) configuration answers 400 itself to a header value with a byte ≥ 0x80, a control byte, obs-fold or whitespace before the colon, so Muntin sees only ASCII. Over cleartext HTTP/2, which the default server also accepts, Flare admits bytes ≥ 0x80, control bytes other than NUL/CR/LF, and names with `:` or bytes ≥ 0x80. Its HPACK decoder replaces invalid UTF-8 with U+FFFD (as Flare does for bodies), so that arrives replaced. The adapter's inbound rule (below) answers 400 to fields Muntin cannot represent (control bytes, non-token names) and keeps valid UTF-8. Arbitrary header bytes are not representable, a stated limitation.

**Raw handlers: R1, fields as raw argument strings.** For a raw route, `App.handle` appends each field's name and value, in order, after the four strings it already passes (method, path, query, body). `_call_raw` rebuilds a `Headers` with `add` (a failure is unreachable, because the fields came from a `Headers`; it would answer the fixed 500) and passes `Request(method, target, body, headers^)`.
- `_Call`, `_Erased`, `_Route`, the storage module and the unsafe surface are unchanged, as is `App.handle(Request) -> Response`'s signature. `App.handle`'s body grows one loop.
- Typed routes get no header strings (extraction is deferred), so their cost is nil.
- Cost on a raw route: one copy of each name and value per request, as bodies already have.
- Measured lossless through production `_Erased`: order across names, casing, `X-B` then `x-b`, empty values, a value with `:` and HTAB, and zero fields (`test_raw_transport_is_lossless_through_erased`). Each name and value is its own list element, so no delimiter is involved.
- R2, passing the `Request` through `_Call`, is rejected without a spike: the storage module must not name `Request` (`check_unsafe.sh`, M2-006), and R1 needs no storage change.

**Responses and defaults: no default headers (D0).** A handler returning `Response`, or a `ToResponse` result building one, sets fields on `response.headers`; a `String` result sets none. `Response.text` adds no `Content-Type`.
- So a response that sets no field keeps its wire headers. (A request whose fields Muntin cannot represent is answered 400 by the inbound rule below; before, the adapter discarded its fields and routed it.) Measured on a scratch copy with the slice applied: `GET /hello` through Flare still carries exactly Flare's own `Content-Length`, `Date` and `Connection: close` and no `Content-Type`, as before.
- A default `Content-Type` belongs with the codec decision (JSON), which needs it.

**Validation: V1, at insertion.** An invalid field fails at the line that adds it. In a raising handler that is a handler error: the fixed 500, or its `ToErrorResponse` (M2-011/M2-013).
- The cost: `add` raises, so non-raising code needs `try`. That includes `ToResponse.to_response`, which must stay non-raising (`headers_fail/add_in_non_raising_code.mojo`; the scratch `Created.to_response` wraps `add`).
- Rejected: V2, a non-raising `add` with `App.handle` checking every response afterwards (`UncheckedHeaders` and `seam_check` in the spike). It removes that `try`, but the error surfaces as an index after the handler returned instead of at the line that added it. (Both need the adapter's re-check below, so the per-response cost does not separate them.)
- Mojo 1.1.0 has no private fields, so `headers._fields.append(...)` bypasses `add` (`headers_known_gaps/internal_field_bypass.mojo`, must build). Fields are one list of pairs, so such a write cannot desynchronize names and values (a pair of lists could, and then crashed on an out-of-bounds index before any check ran). The backend is the second line: the adapter re-checks every outgoing field through `add` and answers the fixed 500 on failure. Flare itself rejects only CR and LF and would emit NUL, other control bytes or a non-token name raw.

**Flare mapping (pinned v0.11.0, measured by `compat/flare/headers/flare_header_probe.mojo`: 49 self-checking observations over HTTP/1.1 and cleartext HTTP/2, the latter with Flare's public HPACK encoder and decoder, run by `check_flare.sh`).**
- **Inbound.** Flare's `HeaderMap` keeps order, original casing, repeated names and empty values, and over HTTP/1.1 trims SP/HTAB around values.
  - It has no public iterator or index access (only `len`, `get`, `get_all`, `contains` by name), so the adapter rebuilds fields from the public `encode_to` (`name: value\r\n` per field) and never reads Flare's `_keys`/`_values`.
  - Over HTTP/1.1 that parses back exactly, because names are tokens. Over cleartext HTTP/2 a name may hold `:` after its first byte, and a parse at the first colon would forge a field: the client's `x-user:admin: zzz` read as `x-user` = `admin: zzz` (found by the review, measured over h2c).
  - The inbound rule therefore verifies the parse against Flare's by-name view. The parsed count must equal `len()`; per name, the parsed values must equal `get_all(name)` position by position; and every field must pass `Headers.add`. Any failure answers 400. Executed in `compat/flare/headers/inbound_rebuild.mojo`: HTTP/1.1-shaped fields rebuild exactly, including UTF-8 and `a: b`; a forged name is rejected alone and beside a real field with the same value; control bytes are rejected, and so is invalid UTF-8 as a lenient HTTP/1.1 configuration would deliver it (over HTTP/2 Flare has already replaced it with U+FFFD).
- **HTTP/2 request rewriting** (measured over h2c): Flare turns `:authority` into a first `Host` field and merges `cookie` fields into one, joined with `"; "`, moved to the end. It refuses (RST_STREAM) uppercase names, a space in a name, NUL/LF and leading or trailing SP/HTAB in values, and `connection`. Muntin receives what Flare's `HeaderMap` holds; these rewrites are Flare's.
- **Outbound.** The adapter appends each field in order with its casing, so repeats, empty values and `Set-Cookie` pairs reach the wire as set (over HTTP/2 Flare lowercases names, as the protocol requires).
  - It omits connection-specific and backend-owned fields on every protocol (`outbound_fields` in the spike):
    - `Content-Length`, `Transfer-Encoding`, `Connection`, `Keep-Alive`, `Proxy-Connection`, `Upgrade` and `TE`, which are connection-specific (RFC 9113 §8.2.2) or framed by the backend;
    - `Trailer`, because Muntin sends no trailers and an announcement of trailers would be false;
    - every field named in a `Connection` value (comma-separated tokens, OWS trimmed, case-insensitive): RFC 9110 §7.6.1 makes them hop-by-hop. Dropping only `Connection` would keep, say, `X-Hop: secret` while erasing the statement that it was connection-only, which changes its meaning.
  - Measured (the probe's `/hopout` and `/hoptoken`). Over HTTP/1.1, Flare drops only `Content-Length`, `Connection` and `Date`: it replaces a handler's `Connection` without reading its tokens, and it sends `Keep-Alive`, `Proxy-Connection`, `Upgrade`, `TE`, `Trailer` and the named `X-Hop`. It also passes `Transfer-Encoding` through beside its own `Content-Length` with an unchunked body, a malformed response. Over HTTP/2, Flare drops `connection`, `transfer-encoding`, `keep-alive`, `proxy-connection` and `upgrade`, but sends `te`, `trailer`, the named `x-hop`, and a handler's `content-length` unchanged (a wrong value with a shorter body is malformed, RFC 9113 §8.1.1), with no stream error. Flare's filters cover a different subset on each protocol, so the adapter applies one rule for both.
  - A handler's `Date` passes through over HTTP/2 and is replaced by Flare's over HTTP/1.1 (Flare-owned; not stripped by the adapter).
  - The fields are stripped, not answered with an error: the backend owns the connection, and Flare's own HTTP/2 path strips the same way.
  - The adapter never uses Flare's `set`, which rewrites only the first case-insensitive match and leaves later duplicates.
  - A `HeaderInjectionError` from Flare (unreachable after the re-check) answers 500.
- **What Flare cannot give Muntin.** Over HTTP/1.1 defaults: nothing a `String` model needs. Over HTTP/2: original name casing (lowercase on the wire), separate `cookie` fields (merged), the position of `:authority`, and fields Muntin rejects (400) rather than represents. Outbound, Muntin can express backend-owned fields that the adapter omits.

**Candidates** (Mojo 1.1.0 (8189361e), Flare v0.11.0):

| Candidate | Result | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| A. `Headers`: ordered fields, original casing, ASCII case-insensitive lookup, validated `add`/`set`, `Optional` `get` | lossless for order, casing, repeats and empty values, through Muntin and through R1; Flare's strict inbound set fits; copies explicitly | **chosen** |
| B. `Dict[lowercase name, List[String]]` | regroups repeated names and loses casing: received `X-B, Content-Type, x-b` is emitted `x-b, x-b, content-type` (`test_candidate_b_loses_order_and_casing`) | rejected |
| C. one comma-joined value per name (RFC 9110 list rule) | `Set-Cookie` is not a list field and its `Expires` contains commas, so two cookies cannot be split back (`test_candidate_c_breaks_set_cookie`) | rejected |
| D. Flare's `HeaderMap` in `Request` | a backend type in the public API (A1), which decides it alone. Also measured: `Movable` only, so the compiler cannot synthesize `Request`'s copy (`compat/flare/headers/headermap_in_request.mojo`; a hand-written copy through `HeaderMap.copy()` would work); `get` returns `""` for absent and empty alike; first-match `set` | rejected |
| V2. non-raising `add`, checked at `App.handle` | the error loses its line; dispatch gains a header check | rejected (V1 chosen) |
| R2. `_Call` carries the `Request` | the storage module would name `Request` (guarded) | rejected (R1 chosen) |

**Typed extraction: deferred.** Typed handlers read no headers in the first slice. A typed result sets headers through `Response`, which is enough for the coherent model above. Header extraction would compete for M3-001's single injected slot (or need a new binding rule), so it needs its own decision. Middleware, CORS and authentication are not designed here.

**Testing.** `TestClient` gains nothing in the first slice. Header tests build `Request(method, target, body, headers^)` and call `App.handle`, the seam `TestClient` uses. In-memory and Flare differ in these cases:
- connection-specific and backend-owned fields, and fields a `Connection` value names, stay in an in-memory `Response` but are omitted on the wire; over HTTP/1.1 a handler's `Date` is replaced by Flare's;
- an internal-name bypass passes in memory but is a 500 on the wire;
- a request field Muntin cannot represent never exists in memory (`Headers.add` rejects it), while over HTTP/2 Flare admits it and the adapter answers 400;
- over HTTP/2, Flare lowercases response names and rewrites request fields as above.

**Evidence.**
- `tests/headers_spike.mojo` + `tests/test_spike_headers.mojo` (12): order, casing and repeats; case-insensitive lookup; empty values; `set`; validation (CR/LF, NUL, DEL, non-token names, leading/trailing SP/HTAB; inner HTAB, `:` and UTF-8 accepted); explicit copies; defaults adding nothing; R1 through production `_Erased`; B's and C's losses; V2's deferred error; the outbound rule (connection-specific and `Connection`-named fields stripped, order kept).
- `tests/headers_lib_only/driver.mojo`: built by `check.sh` without the application module.
- `tests/headers_fail` (5): borrowed request, `add` in non-raising code, implicit copy, `Optional` `get`, headers moved into `Request` (`^` required).
- `tests/headers_known_gaps/internal_field_bypass.mojo` (must build).
- `compat/flare/headers/flare_header_probe.mojo` (49 observations over raw loopback TCP: HTTP/1.1 on default and obs-text-lenient servers, cleartext HTTP/2 on the default server, hop-by-hop response fields on both), `inbound_rebuild.mojo` (the inbound rule, 3 tests) and `headermap_in_request.mojo` (must not build), all in `check_flare.sh`.
- Mutations (planted, reverted; 16), each red: in the spike, names lowercased on store; dedupe on add; Flare-style first-match `set`; case-sensitive lookup; an empty value treated as absent; R1 dropping empty values; R1 reversing order; value validation removed; HTAB rejected; leading/trailing whitespace allowed; a default header on `Request`; in the inbound rule, the verification against `len`/`get_all` removed (the forged name and invalid UTF-8 get through); in the outbound rule, `Connection`-named fields kept, `Upgrade` kept, `TE`/`Trailer` kept, `Connection` tokens untrimmed.
- Scratch copy of this branch with the slice applied (not retained): `Headers` in `http.mojo` and exported, the two fields, R1 in `App.handle` and `_call_raw`, the Flare mapping above.
  - `./scripts/check.sh`, `./scripts/test.sh` (every suite's count unchanged, plus 4 scratch tests) and `./scripts/check_flare.sh` (plus one loopback test) exit 0.
  - The 90 production fixtures' diagnostics (`compile_fail`, `storage_fail`, `body_fail`, `state_get_fail`) are identical.
  - Over HTTP/1.1 loopback, `X-Signature` and repeated `X-A`/`x-a` reach a raw handler. Two `Set-Cookie`, an empty value and `X-Request-Id` come back. `Transfer-Encoding` is omitted. An injection attempt is 500 in memory and on the wire, and an internal-list bypass is 500 on the wire. (The scratch copy predates the review's h2c finding. Its inbound mapping split at the first colon without verification; the inbound rule above replaces it and is executed in `inbound_rebuild.mojo`.)

**Revisit when:**
- typed header extraction is proposed: decide its binding (it competes for the single injected slot) before adding it;
- a codec (JSON) is decided: decide the default `Content-Type` and whether `Response.text` gains one (that changes existing wire bytes);
- `ToResponse` implementations that set headers prove common: consider a non-raising way to add fields known valid;
- Flare exposes a public field iterator: map inbound fields through it instead of parsing `encode_to`;
- a backend or Flare configuration accepts obs-text: the U+FFFD replacement becomes observable, so decide whether bytes need a representation;
- Mojo gets private fields: the internal-name bypass closes, and the adapter's re-check can be reconsidered;
- `flare_header_probe.mojo` fails: Flare's header behavior changed; re-measure the mapping;
- Flare's HTTP/2 path validates names as tokens, or exposes a public field iterator: the inbound verification can be simplified;
- Muntin gains trailers or streaming responses: decide `Trailer` and `TE` instead of omitting them.

**Next production slice (M3-005): headers in production.** Implemented as listed ("Headers in production (M3-005)" below).
- `src/muntin/http.mojo`: `Headers` as in the spike (validation helpers private), `Request.headers` with the defaulted `var headers` initializer argument, and `Response.headers`. `muntin` exports `Headers`.
- `src/muntin/app.mojo`: in `App.handle`, a raw route appends each field's name and value after the four strings. `_call_raw` rebuilds `Headers` with `add`; a failure is the fixed 500. Typed routes are unchanged.
- `adapters/flare/muntin_flare.mojo`:
  - inbound fields rebuilt from `encode_to` and verified as in `inbound_rebuild.mojo` (count equals `len()`, per-name values equal `get_all` by position with ASCII case-insensitive names, each passes `add`); any failure answers 400. The check is quadratic in the number of fields, which Flare's 8 KB header limit bounds;
  - outbound fields filtered by the outbound rule (`Content-Length`, `Transfer-Encoding`, `Connection`, `Keep-Alive`, `Proxy-Connection`, `Upgrade`, `TE`, `Trailer` and every `Connection`-named field omitted), appended in order, each re-checked through `add`, with a failure or `HeaderInjectionError` answering 500;
  - the module comment updated.
- Unchanged: `_Call`, `_Erased`, `_Route`, the storage module, the unsafe surface, `App.handle`'s signature, `TestClient`, `Response.text` (no default headers) and every existing test, fixture and expected text.
- Tests: the spike's semantics on production `Headers`; raw handlers reading and writing headers through `App.handle`; a typed `ToResponse` result setting a header; existing routes setting none. Loopback through Flare covers request fields (order, casing, repeats, empty values), response fields (`Set-Cookie` twice, an empty value), `Transfer-Encoding` omitted, an injection attempt as 500, `GET /hello`'s headers unchanged, over h2c a forged `x-user:admin` name answered 400, and `Connection: X-Hop` with `X-Hop` omitting both on HTTP/1.1 and h2c. Production fixtures counterpart to `tests/headers_fail`.
- Docs: DX section 9's webhook reads `req.headers.get("x-signature")`, and DX gains a headers section.
- Not in the slice: typed header extraction, a default `Content-Type`, `TestClient` header methods, middleware, CORS and authentication.

### Headers in production (M3-005)

Status: **production**, exactly the M3-002 slice. Decision, semantics and Flare measurements: "Headers decision (M3-002)" above.
- `src/muntin/http.mojo`: `Headers` (one list of `_Field` pairs; validation helpers `_valid_name`, `_valid_value` and ASCII `_same_name` are private) and the `headers` fields of `Request` (defaulted `var headers` initializer argument) and `Response`. `muntin` exports `Headers`.
- `src/muntin/app.mojo`: `App.handle` appends each field's name and value after a raw route's four strings, and `_call_raw` rebuilds `Headers` with `add` (failure: the fixed 500). Typed routes, `_Call`, `_Erased`, `_Route`, the storage module, the unsafe surface, `App.handle`'s signature and `TestClient` are unchanged.
- `adapters/flare/muntin_flare.mojo`: `to_muntin_headers` rebuilds and verifies inbound fields (400 on failure, in `MuntinHandler.serve`); `to_flare_response` applies the outbound rule and re-checks each field (500 on failure).
- Unchanged behavior: every existing test, fixture and expected text. The 90 production fixtures' diagnostics are identical to `main`'s. For a request whose fields Muntin can represent, a response that sets no field keeps its previous wire headers, since `Response.text` adds none (`GET /hello` over loopback still carries only Flare's three fields).
- Changed behavior: a request Flare accepts but Muntin cannot represent (such as a forged `x-user:admin` over h2c) was routed with its fields discarded and is now answered 400 before `App.handle`.
- Evidence:
  - `tests/test_headers.mojo` (13): the spike's semantics on production `Headers`; raw handlers reading and writing fields through `App.handle`; every field kept through the raw transport; typed routes and `String` results setting none; a typed `ToResponse` result setting `Location`; an invalid field as the fixed 500; DX section 9's headers example.
  - `tests/headers_api_fail` (5): `tests/headers_fail`'s invariants on production types.
  - `adapters/flare/test_muntin_flare.mojo` (+5, 13 in all): inbound fields rebuilt in order; a forged `x-user:admin` (alone and beside a real field) and a control byte answered 400; the outbound rule; an internal-name write answered 500; no fields added to a response without any.
  - `adapters/flare/test_localhost_roundtrip.mojo` (+2): over HTTP/1.1, `X-Signature` and repeated `X-A`/`x-a` reach a raw handler; `X-Request-Id`, two `Set-Cookie` and an empty value come back; `Transfer-Encoding`, `Keep-Alive`, `Upgrade` and the `Connection`-named `X-Hop` are omitted on the wire but kept in memory; an injection attempt is 500 on both. Over cleartext HTTP/2 the same route answers its fields in order (lowercase) without the omitted ones, and a forged `x-user:admin` name is answered 400.
  - Mutations (planted, reverted; 15), each red: in `Headers`, names lowercased, dedupe on `add`, first-match `set`, case-sensitive names, an empty value treated as absent, the whitespace rule removed; in `app.mojo`, no header strings for raw routes, `_call_raw` dropping fields; a default header on `Request`, a default `Content-Type` on `Response`; in the adapter, inbound verification removed, an inbound `add` failure skipped, `Connection`-named fields kept, `Upgrade` kept, the outbound re-check removed.

### Stateful POST in production (M3-006)

Status: **production**, the `post` half of the M3-001 typed family on M3-004's storage. Decision: "Application state decision (M3-001)"; storage: "State storage decision (M3-004)"; the `get` half: M3-003.

- `src/muntin/app.mojo`: two adapters, `_call_state_body[S, B, E, R, respond]` and `_call_state_int_body[S, B, E, R, respond]`, each `_call_body`/`_call_int_body` with `bound.state` passed first by borrow (the spike's, unchanged in logic), and four `App.post` overloads taking `(handler, state: State[S])`:
  - `def(State[S], var B) thin raises E -> String` and `-> R: ToResponse`;
  - `def(State[S], Int, var B) thin raises E -> String` and `-> R: ToResponse`.
  `S`, `B`, `E` and `R` are inferred as in their stateless twins; each repeats its twin's route checks and messages. They are placed after the raw `post`, as the stateful `get` overloads follow the raw `get`. A registration stores the handler and one copy of the handle as M3-003's `_Bound[H, S]` in `_Erased`, with `body=True`; requests borrow it.
- The `State` guard (M3-001's "Disjointness kept", deferred by M3-003): `State` conforms to a private marker trait, `_InjectedState` (`src/muntin/state.mojo`; no requirement, no field). The four stateless body overloads assert `not conforms_to(B, _InjectedState)` after the `Int` and `Request` guards and before `FromBody` ("State is injected application state, not the request body; a stateful post handler takes State first and the body last, and the state is the registration's second argument"); the four stateful ones assert it in the body slot ("a handler takes at most one State, as its first parameter"). Like the `Request` guard, it changes only the message of calls that already fail; selection is unchanged (argument count still separates the families). It is not a dependency-injection abstraction: nothing else conforms, and nothing reads it but these asserts.
- Unchanged: `_Erased`, `_Call`, `_Route`, `_handler_storage.mojo` (`_Shared`), `check_unsafe.sh` and the unsafe surface, `State`'s fields and public API (`State(value)`, `.copy()`, `state[]`), `_Bound`, the stateful `get` overloads and adapters, `App.handle`, `http.mojo`, `body.mojo`, `testing.mojo`, `adapters/flare/muntin_flare.mojo`. `App` stays non-generic.
- Behavior: the stateless twin's. Route value first (400 without calling `from_body`), then the body (`from_body` raising is 400 without calling the handler), then the handler once and the result policy once; a raise is `ToErrorResponse` or the fixed 500; 404 without a match; first registration wins, across the stateless and stateful families. Typed routes receive no header strings (M3-005), so request fields take no part.
- Lifetime: each registration raises the handle's count by one; requests of every shape and outcome leave it unchanged, read inside handlers of both shapes; dropping the `App` releases the routes' handles; the value is dropped once across two `App` moves.
- Diagnostics: of the 178 existing must-fail fixtures (all directories, built against production), 170 are identical. The 8 that change are failing one-argument `post` calls with no viable overload (`body_fail/post_body_then_int`, `post_int_and_two_bodies`, `post_int_body_non_conforming_return`, `post_no_param`, `post_non_conforming_return`, `post_owned_int_and_body`, `post_two_bodies`, `raw_extra_parameter`): each gains the four stateful candidates' `missing required argument: 'state'` notes, the error line's caret moves from the handler argument to `post`, and four of them lose trailing detail notes to Mojo's ten-note cap (the compiler reports 3, 3, 1 and 4 omitted for `post_body_then_int`, `post_owned_int_and_body`, `post_two_bodies` and `raw_extra_parameter`; the lost notes are `result type.origin ...`, `.id of the first type ...` and `result type of the first type ...` details). Every expected text still matches. `get` diagnostics are unchanged. One message changes for a call outside the slice: a stateful raw handler `def(State[S], Request) -> Response` on `post` with its state reaches the stateful `ToResponse` body overload (`B = Request`) and gets the `Request` guard message (`state_post_fail/raw_stateful_post.mojo`, retired by M3-007, which made the shape production).
- Evidence:
  - `tests/test_state_post.mojo` (12): both shapes and results; state, route value and body each reaching their own parameter (path and query); a bad body as 400 with the handler not called; a bad, missing or duplicated route value as 400 with `from_body` not called; 404s; `ToErrorResponse`, a bare `raises` and an application type without `ToErrorResponse` (fixed 500); first registration wins; stateless `post` beside stateful routes; the reference count per registration (all four overloads), inside handlers of all four overloads and across requests; drop once across two `App` moves; `TestClient` before and after an `App` move; request fields taking no part; DX section 8's `post` example.
  - `tests/state_post_fail` (13): a missing state (both shapes), the value instead of a handle, a plain `Db` parameter, a mismatched state type, the state after the body and after the route value, `var` and `mut` state parameters, a state for a stateless handler, a `def(State[S])` handler with no body, mutation inside a handler, the stateful raw handler above. `tests/compile_fail/state_post_*.mojo` (10): placeholder counts on both shapes and results, a malformed route, a body type without `FromBody`, `Int` as the body, a second `State` as the body. `tests/compile_fail/post_*state_as_body.mojo` (4): `def(State[Db])` and `def(Int, State[Db])` on stateless `post`, both results, with the new guard message.
  - `adapters/flare/test_localhost_roundtrip.mojo`: stateful `POST /staff` (`-> Person`, `raises OutOfStock`) and `POST /staff/{id}` (`-> String`) share `GET /staff/{id}`'s `State[Staff]`; success, a bad body (400), a bad route value (400), the application error (409) and an unmatched path (404) over loopback equal `TestClient`. Ordering (400 before `from_body` or the handler) is not observable from the parent of the forked server; `tests/test_state_post.mojo` proves it.
  - Mutations (planted, reverted; 16: 15 through `TestClient` and the fixtures, plus the index swap again over loopback), each red. State binding: a stateful body-only route registered as `GET`; a stateful route-value-then-body route without `body=True` (crash). Argument order and extraction: route value and body indices swapped in `_call_state_int_body` (also red over loopback); the body converted before the route value; the route value altered before the handler. Body extraction: a `from_body` failure answered 500. Handler errors: a body-only handler error answered 400; a route-value-then-body handler error always the fixed 500. Per-request copying: the handle copied per request in each adapter (the count read inside handlers rises). Diagnostics: `State` without the marker, the stateless guard removed from two overloads, the stateful guard removed (each fixture loses its expected text); a stateful overload's placeholder-count assert removed (the fixture compiles).
- Fresh-context review: no material issue. Minors applied: the stateless guard message names the stateful shape (it said only "pass the state as the registration's second argument", which fails for a handler with no body or with the state after the route value); the mutation count is itemized; the reference count is also read inside `ToResponse` handlers of both shapes; the module comment no longer says "exactly as its stateless twin".

### Stateful raw handlers in production (M3-007)

Status: **production**, the raw member of the M3-001 family on M3-004's storage; with M3-003 and M3-006 it completes the stateful handler family. Decision: "Application state decision (M3-001)" (the *Raw* shape and *Inside the family*); raw semantics: "Raw Request handlers in production (M2-015)" and "Headers in production (M3-005)".

- `src/muntin/app.mojo`: one adapter, `_call_state_raw[S, E]`, which is `_call_raw` with `bound.state` passed first by borrow, and two overloads, `App.get` and `App.post`, each `[S, E, //, path]` taking `(handler: def(State[S], var Request) thin raises E -> Response, state: State[S])`. Each is placed last in its method's group and repeats the raw twin's route checks and messages. A registration stores the handler and one copy of the handle as M3-003's `_Bound[H, S]` in `_Erased`, with `raw=True`; requests borrow it. `_call_raw`'s request rebuild moved into `_raw_request(args) raises -> Request`, which both raw adapters call; a rebuild failure is still the fixed 500, and `tests/test_raw.mojo` and `tests/test_headers.mojo` pass unchanged.
- Selection: on `post` a stateful raw handler also satisfies the stateful `ToResponse` body overload (`B = Request`, `R = Response`). The raw overload's list `[S, E, path]` (3) is shorter than `[S, B, E, R, path]` (5), so M2-014's rule selects it; the `String` body overload is not viable for a `Response` result. Mutation: two defaulted parameters on the stateful raw `post` make the lists equal, and every such call becomes `ambiguous call to 'post'`. On `get` no other stateful overload's handler type has a `Request` parameter. The argument count separates stateless and stateful raw routes, as for the typed shapes.
- The stateful body overloads' `Request` guard names the stateful raw shape: `Request is the whole request, not a body; a stateful raw handler takes State first, then only the Request, and returns Response`. It used the stateless text (`a raw handler takes only the Request and returns Response`), which pointed a stateful handler at a shape without its state. The stateless overloads keep their text. It changes only messages: selection is unchanged, and no existing fixture expected the old text from a stateful overload (the retired `state_post_fail/raw_stateful_post.mojo` was the only one).
- Unchanged: `_Erased`, `_Call`, `_Route`, `_handler_storage.mojo` (`_Shared`), `check_unsafe.sh` and the unsafe surface, `State` and its marker, `_Bound`, the typed and stateful typed overloads and adapters, `App.handle`, `http.mojo`, `body.mojo`, `testing.mojo`, `adapters/flare/muntin_flare.mojo`. `App` stays non-generic.
- Note budget (revisit condition): `get` and `post` now have ten overloads each, and Mojo 1.1.0 shows at most ten notes. A failing one-argument call shows ten candidate notes and no detail note. One more overload on either method (for example raw route values or raw `String`/`ToResponse` results, which SPEC lists as candidates) would drop candidate notes themselves, so DX's "one note per candidate" would stop holding. Such a slice must measure its diagnostics against this budget.
- Behavior: the raw twin's, with the state first. The handler receives the whole `Request`, moved in as raw handlers receive it: method, path, the query undecoded, the body (empty included) and the header fields in order with their casing and repeats. No typed extraction runs (no query gathering, `_parse_int` or `from_body`), so Muntin answers no 400 after the match. The `Response` is the answer, unconverted; its status, body and fields survive, and the Flare adapter applies its outbound rule to the fields. A raise is `ToErrorResponse` or the fixed 500; 404 without a match; the first registration wins across stateless raw, stateful raw and typed routes, and a typed route's 400 does not fall through. Route literals stay static.
- Lifetime: each registration raises the handle's count by one; requests on both methods leave it unchanged, read inside handlers and across successful, 401, 500 and 404 requests; dropping the `App` releases the routes' handles; the value is dropped once across two `App` moves.
- Diagnostics: of the 204 existing must-fail fixtures (all directories, built against production; `raw_stateful_post` retired), 171 are identical. In the 33 that change, every error line and caret is identical; only notes change:
  - 19 failing one-argument calls with no viable overload gain a fifth `missing required argument: 'state'` note (`body_fail`: `get_body_handler`, `post_body_then_int`, `post_int_and_two_bodies`, `post_int_body_non_conforming_return`, `post_no_param`, `post_non_conforming_return`, `post_owned_int_and_body`, `post_two_bodies`, `raw_extra_parameter`; `storage_fail`: `error_type_is_not_a_result`, `linear_error_type`, `non_conforming_return_handler`, `raw_get_string_return`, `request_param_handler`, `string_param_handler`, `two_param_handler`; `state_get_fail/missing_state_argument`; `state_post_fail/missing_state_argument`, `missing_state_argument_int_body`);
  - 14 failing two-argument calls gain the stateful raw candidate's note (`state_get_fail`: `mismatched_state_type`, `owned_state_parameter`, `plain_value_as_state`, `state_for_stateless_handler`, `state_last`; `state_post_fail`: `mismatched_state_type`, `mut_state_parameter`, `owned_state_parameter`, `plain_value_as_state`, `plain_value_parameter`, `state_after_body`, `state_after_route_value`, `state_for_stateless_handler`, `state_without_body`);
  - 20 of the 33 lose one trailing detail note to Mojo's ten-note cap (`... of the first type is ... but the second type is ...` or `result type.origin ...`; never a candidate's note): `body_fail/get_body_handler`, `post_body_then_int`, `post_int_and_two_bodies`, `post_owned_int_and_body`, `post_two_bodies`, `raw_extra_parameter`; `storage_fail/error_type_is_not_a_result`, `linear_error_type`, `raw_get_string_return`, `request_param_handler`, `string_param_handler`, `two_param_handler`; `state_get_fail/mismatched_state_type`, `missing_state_argument`, `state_last`; `state_post_fail/mismatched_state_type`, `missing_state_argument`, `missing_state_argument_int_body`, `mut_state_parameter`, `state_after_route_value`.
  Every expected text still matches.
- Evidence:
  - `tests/test_state_raw.mojo` (13, through `App.handle` and `TestClient`): stateful raw `GET` and `POST`; method, path, query (adversarial), body and header fields reaching the handler unchanged beside the state in the same call; borrowed and `var` requests (the caller's request untouched); an explicit function value; no typed extraction (`from_body` not called; stateful typed routes beside it do answer 400); response status, body and fields; a returned 400; `ToErrorResponse` decided from the state and a header, bare `raises` and an application type without `ToErrorResponse` (fixed 500, no error text); 404s with the handler not called; first registration wins across stateless raw, stateful raw and typed routes on both methods; the reference count per registration, inside handlers and across successful and failing requests; drop once across two `App` moves; `TestClient` equal to `App.handle` before and after a move; typed, stateful typed and stateless raw routes unchanged beside it (typed routes still get no header strings); DX section 8's stateful raw example.
  - `tests/state_raw_fail` (26, 13 per method): a missing state, the value instead of a handle, a plain `Db` parameter, a mismatched state type, the state after the `Request`, `var` and `mut` state parameters, an extra parameter, an `Int` before the `Request`, a `String` and a `ToResponse` result, a state for a stateless raw handler, mutation inside a handler. On `post` an `Int` before the `Request` and a non-`Response` result reach a stateful body overload with `B = Request` and get the guard above; on `get` they get `no matching method in call to 'get'` with the stateful raw candidate's note. `tests/compile_fail/state_raw_*` (6): a path placeholder, a query placeholder and a malformed literal on each method.
  - `adapters/flare/test_localhost_roundtrip.mojo`: stateful raw `GET /keyed` and `POST /keyed` on `headers_app()` read `X-Signature` and the repeated `X-A`, compare the signature with the state, echo the state and the whole request, and set `X-Request-Id`, two `Set-Cookie` and `Keep-Alive`. Over HTTP/1.1 status, body and the kept fields in order equal `App.handle` (`Keep-Alive` omitted on the wire, kept in memory); a missing signature (also through `TestClient`) and a wrong one are `Unsigned`'s 401; `PUT /keyed` and `GET /keyed/x` are 404. The adapter is unchanged.
  - What the fixtures prove: each call is rejected with its expected text. Some do not prove the new overloads exist: with a stateful raw overload deleted, 8 of the `post` fixtures and 2 of the `get` ones still fail with the same text, because a stateful body overload gives the same `state` note or `Request` guard, or because the error is in the handler body (`*_mutate_in_handler`). The positive tests prove the overloads (a deleted one fails `tests/test_state_raw.mojo`).
  - Mutations (planted, reverted; 14), each red. State binding and methods: the stateful raw `get` registered as `POST`, the `post` as `GET`, the `get` without `raw=True` (crash). Request transport: the rebuilt request without its headers, query or body, or built from the path alone. Errors: a handler error answered 400, or always the fixed 500. Per-request copying: the handle copied per request (the count read inside handlers rises). Selection: two defaulted parameters on the stateful raw `post` (`ambiguous call to 'post'`). Diagnostics: the stateful guard reverted to the stateless text (three `state_raw_fail` fixtures lose their expected text). Over loopback: the dropped headers and the always-500 errors.
- Fresh-context review: no material issue (overload probes with `S = Request`, `S = Int`, a `FromBody` type as state, keyword arguments and `raises T` dispatch correctly; the selection mutation, the 33 changed diagnostics and the `_raw_request` refactor reproduced; storage, unsafe surface and adapter diffs empty). Minors applied: the note-budget revisit condition and what the fixtures prove (above); DX's notes for a wrong state type or a value instead of a handle; the explicit function-value spelling in DX section 8; the `_Route.raw` and `App.handle` docstrings name both raw adapters (comments only); the `check.sh` comment for `tests/state_raw_fail`.

### JSON codec decision (M3-008)

Status: **decision** (M3-008; production and adapters are unchanged in it; production since M3-009, "JSON in production (M3-009)" below). It decides Muntin's first JSON API, who owns the codec, the request and response `Content-Type` rules, where JSON failures go in the error model, and the production slice that follows.

**Selected: A, a Muntin-owned `Json[T]` wrapper over application `FromJson`/`ToJson` traits, with a Muntin-owned codec: a strict parser for the RFC 8259 grammar with Muntin-specific duplicate-name, depth, size and numeric limits, and a writer.** `Json[T]` is a body through the existing `FromBody` and a result through the existing `ToResponse`, so no `App` overload, adapter shape or storage changes for it to register.

```mojo
trait FromJson(Deinitable, Movable):
    @staticmethod
    def from_json(value: JsonValue) raises -> Self       # raise -> 400

trait ToJson:
    def write_json(self, mut out: JsonWriter) raises     # raise -> fixed 500 (serialization failure)

struct Json[T: Movable & Deinitable](
    Deinitable, Movable,
    FromBody where conforms_to(T, FromJson),
    ToResponse where conforms_to(T, ToJson),
    _JsonBody,                                            # private marker: the Content-Type step
):
    var value: T
    def __init__(out self, var value: T)
    def take(deinit self) -> T                            # body^.take(): moves the value out

struct JsonValue(Copyable, Movable, Sized):               # a read-only position in a parsed document; made only by the parser and accessors
    def is_null(self) -> Bool
    def bool(self) raises -> Bool
    def int(self) raises -> Int                           # integer literal (no fraction/exponent) that fits Int
    def float(self) raises -> Float64                     # a number literal through Mojo 1.1.0 `atof` (finite; see Parsing)
    def string(self) raises -> String
    def __len__(self) -> Int                              # elements or members; 0 for scalars
    def __getitem__(self, index: Int) raises -> JsonValue # array element, constant time
    def __getitem__(self, name: String) raises -> JsonValue  # object member; raises when absent
    def get(self, name: String) raises -> Optional[JsonValue]  # None when absent; null is a value

struct JsonWriter(Movable):
    def begin_object / end_object / begin_array / end_array (mut self) raises
    def name(mut self, name: String) raises               # a name repeated in one object raises at end_object
    def null / bool / int / float / string (mut self, ...) raises   # float raises on NaN/infinity
    def value[T: ToJson](mut self, value: T) raises       # a nested application value
```

```mojo
def create_user(body: Json[CreateUser]) -> Json[User]:   # or `var body` and body^.take()
    return Json(User(1, body.value.name))

app.post["/users"](create_user)                          # existing overload; unchanged syntax
```

- **Opt-in is explicit, per type.** The application declares `FromJson` and/or `ToJson` on its own types and maps fields itself; Muntin never names them. `Json[T]` conforms to `FromBody` only when `T: FromJson` and to `ToResponse` only when `T: ToJson` (documented conditional conformance with `where`-gated methods, Mojo manual "Conditional trait conformance"). A wrong `T` fails at the registration with the existing messages (`json_fail/body_without_from_json`: the production `FromBody` assert; `result_without_to_json`: `argument type 'Json[In]' does not conform to trait 'ToResponse'`).
- **Ownership.** `Json` moves its value in (`Json(v^)`) and out (`body^.take()`, `deinit self`). A field cannot be moved out of the middle of a `Json` on Mojo 1.1.0 (`json_fail/move_value_out_of_json`), so `take` exists. Move-only `T` works as body and as result (`test_move_only_body_and_result`). A handler may borrow (`body: Json[T]`, read `body.value`) or own (`var body`).
- **Parsed document.** Mojo 1.1.0 rejects a struct that holds a list of itself (`json_fail/recursive_value_tree`), so a document is a flat tape and `JsonValue` is a position in it. The tape owns no text: a node (28 bytes) holds its kind, flags and byte spans into the document's one copy of the body (a number's literal, a string's contents and a member's name, escapes decoded on access), and each container's child node indices sit contiguously in one table, so `value[i]` is constant time and an index loop is linear. Duplicate member names are found by sorting each object's decoded names and comparing neighbours (O(m log m) per object and deterministic; a hash set would be linear only for a seed an attacker cannot predict, and Mojo 1.1.0's `String` hash is the same in every run, found by the review). Parsing is otherwise linear in the body (`test_parsing_and_access_are_linear`: an 80k-member object and a 200k-element array read by index, both under the 1 MiB cap; the first spike's list scans took 1.1 s at 40k members and 8.8 s for an 80k-element index loop, found by the review). The tape is held in M3-004's sealed `_Shared` box: copies of a `JsonValue` share it, and no copy can clear or replace it on an ordinary path (`json_fail/second_handle_mutates_document`; with `ArcPointer`, `v2._doc[].nodes.clear()` compiles and empties the tape another position indexes, measured). Accessors return values, never references into the tape. `JsonValue`'s initializer takes keyword-only internal arguments (`_doc=`, `_at=`): positions come from the parser and accessors, and an out-of-range position traps on the list bounds check rather than reading other memory. This adds no unsafe code outside `_handler_storage.mojo` (`check_unsafe.sh` already allows importing `_Shared`).
- **No `rebind`, no reflection, no `__extension` in the selected codec.** It uses safe std types, `conforms_to` and conditional conformance only.

**Codec source.** Mojo 1.1.0's standard library has no JSON module (`import std.json`: `unable to locate module 'json'`). The only JSON package on the pinned lock is `json` vendored inside Flare's conda package (`lib/mojo/json`, with `lib/libsimdjson_wrapper.so`), so it exists only in the `flare` environment (`json_fail/flare_json_package_in_default_env`), its reflection serde relies on `__extension` (undocumented retroactive conformance, never a supported opt-in here) and `MaybeUninit`, and depending on it would make core depend on the backend's package (A2). Flare's own JSON extractors (`flare/http/extract.mojo`) are not used either. So Muntin owns a small codec: no new dependency, and it is replaceable later behind `FromJson`/`ToJson`, whose requirements name only Muntin types.

**Parsing (the RFC 8259 grammar, strictly, with Muntin's limits; the slice's supported set).** RFC 8259 recommends unique member names without making duplicates a grammar error, and sets no depth or size limit; rejecting duplicates, nesting past 64 and bodies over 1 MiB, and the numeric gaps below, are Muntin's profile, not RFC requirements.
- Accepted: one JSON text of any kind (objects, arrays, strings, numbers, `true`, `false`, `null`), with SP/HTAB/LF/CR whitespace; string escapes `\" \\ \/ \b \f \n \r \t \uXXXX`, surrogate pairs combined; nesting up to 64 arrays/objects (`MAX_DEPTH`); unknown object members are kept and ignored unless `from_json` reads them.
- Rejected (400 through `from_body`): an empty body, trailing content or a second text, comments, trailing commas, single quotes, unquoted names, leading zeros, `+`, `.5`, `1.`, `NaN`/`Infinity`, a byte order mark, unescaped control bytes in strings, bad escapes, lone surrogates, duplicate member names (parser differentials), nesting past 64.
- Field access: a missing member, a wrong kind, `int()` on `1.5`/`1e2` or beyond `Int`, `float()` overflowing to infinity all raise, so `from_json` raises and the request is 400. Absent and `null` are told apart (`get` -> `None` vs `is_null()`), which is how an `Optional` field is read.
- Numbers through Mojo 1.1.0: `atof` is lenient (it accepts `1.`, `.5`, `01`, `+1`, ` 1`, `nan`, `inf`), so the parser validates the RFC grammar itself. `atof` raises on long literals, by length and magnitude rather than significant digits (`123456789012345678901234`, `100000000000000000000000`, `0.000000000000000000000000000001`), so `float()` answers 400 for those valid numbers. And `atof` is not always correctly rounded: shortest round-trip doubles as JavaScript and Python clients send them (`-2.7546748226290886e+20`, 2 of 2997 random doubles in the review) and long integer literals read as floats (`123456789012345678`; 447 of 2000 16–19-digit integers in the review) come back 1 ulp off, silently. `int()` is exact. All are pinned (`test_number_limits_on_mojo_1_1_0`, `test_float_rounding_gaps_on_mojo_1_1_0`); the decision accepts them as Mojo 1.1.0 limits of `float()` rather than ship a Muntin float parser, and an application that needs exact values reads integers with `int()`.
- Invalid UTF-8 is not representable in `Request.body` (a `String`); through Flare it arrives replaced by U+FFFD (the M2-016 fact, measured again in `compat/flare/json/json_loopback_probe.mojo`), so the codec cannot reject it.

**Writing.** `JsonWriter` emits separators itself and raises on structural misuse (a value without a name in an object, a name outside one, a name repeated in one object, which Muntin's own parser would reject (found at `end_object` by sorting the object's names, as the parser does: names may come from request data, so the writer depends on no predictable hash), an unmatched close, a second top-level value, an unfinished document) and on NaN or infinity, which JSON cannot represent (`String(Float64)` prints `nan`/`inf`). Strings escape `"`, `\` and every byte below 0x20 (`\n`, `\r`, `\t`, else `\u00XX`); other bytes are written as the UTF-8 they are. Finite floats use `String(Float64)`, which is valid JSON (`1e+16`, `1e-07`, `5e-324`, `-150.0`) but, on Mojo 1.1.0, not always the text of the same double: bits `0xc360b3b71251310b` print as `-3.760958796054742e+16`, which reads back 1 ulp off (7 of 6999 in the review; pinned).

**Content-Type.**
- Responses: `Json[T].to_response()` answers 200 with exactly one field, `Content-Type: application/json` (no `charset`: RFC 8259 defines none). `String` results and `Response.text` still add no field (`test_text_results_keep_no_default_fields`), so no existing wire byte changes. Override: the application converts and edits, `var r = Json(v^).to_response(); r.status = 201; r.headers.set("Content-Type", "application/problem+json")`, returning `Response`; a `set` after the conversion wins and Muntin never re-adds or checks the field. The adapter's outbound rule does not touch `Content-Type` (measured over loopback).
- Requests: a `Json[T]` body requires exactly one `Content-Type` field whose media type, the text before any `;` with SP/HTAB trimmed, is `application/json`, compared ASCII case-insensitively byte by byte (Unicode case folding is not used: `applİcation/json` is 415). Parameters are not interpreted (`application/json; charset=utf-8` is accepted; the body is read as UTF-8 per RFC 8259 §8.1).
