A missing field, two fields, `text/plain`, `application/x-www-form-urlencoded`, `application/problem+json` and other `+json` types are 415 `Unsupported Media Type` (body `Unsupported Media Type`, no fields), before JSON parsing and before the handler. (The body has already been received: the backend built `Request.body` before `App.handle`; the step avoids parsing it, not receiving it.) Why require it: browsers send cross-site "simple" requests without a preflight with `text/plain`, a form type, or no `Content-Type` at all (a `Blob` body with an empty type), the known CSRF path into JSON endpoints that accept any type; and starting strict is reversible, since accepting `+json` or a missing field later is additive while tightening later breaks deployed clients. Why 415 rather than 400: it is the status for this condition (RFC 9110 §15.5.16), and it keeps "the body is not valid JSON" (400) distinguishable. Other bodies are unchanged: an application `FromBody` type still sees no headers and requires no `Content-Type`.
- `FromBody.from_body(String)` cannot see headers, so the step is the body adapters', keyed on the private marker `_JsonBody` (as M3-006 keys the `State` guard on `_InjectedState`). Order on a JSON body route: 404 → query value 400 (`App.handle`) → route value 400 (adapter) → 415 (adapter) → 413 (adapter, body cap below) → JSON 400 (`from_body`: parse or `from_json`) → handler → result conversion. Measured on the spike's mirror (`model_post`, `model_post_int`; `test_selected_order_on_the_adapter_mirror`, `test_body_size_cap`): a malformed body with a wrong type is 415, a bad `Int` segment with a wrong type is 400, an oversized body is 415 with a wrong type, 400 with a bad `Int` segment and 413 otherwise.
- M2 stays closed: the M2 contract's "a request failure ... `from_body` raise is 400" is about application-defined `B: FromBody`, and every such route keeps its 400/404/500 boundaries; 415 and 413 exist only for `Json[T]`, a new Muntin-owned body type.
- M3-005 is unchanged: `Headers` validation, the adapter's inbound rule and its outbound filtering apply to `Content-Type` as to any field (the codec sets it with `Headers.set`, and `Content-Type` is not an omitted field).

**Body cap: a fixed 1 MiB for JSON request bodies, 413 above it.**
- `MAX_BODY_BYTES = 1_048_576`, compared with the body's byte length. A typed JSON route answers a larger body 413 `Content Too Large` (body `Content Too Large`, no fields; RFC 9110 §15.5.14) after the 415 step and before `from_body`, so nothing is parsed. Only `Json[T]` bodies are capped; application `FromBody` types and raw handlers keep whatever the backend delivers.
- The parser enforces the same cap: `Json[T].from_body` raises on a larger body wherever it is called, so a raw handler that decodes with it gets an error (its own error model applies), never an unbounded parse. Through `from_body` alone the answer would be 400, because `FromBody`'s raise is the request-side 400; that is why typed routes need the adapter step for 413 (`test_body_size_cap`: production `App` without the step answers 400).
- Why a cap in Muntin: the codec is reachable without any backend (`App.handle`, `TestClient`, `Json[T].from_body`), and a parse costs up to about 37 bytes of memory per body byte. At the cap the measured worst case (`[0,0,...]`, 1 MiB) adds 28 MB of peak memory and parses in 20 ms; a 1 MiB array of typical records adds 4.8 MB. `App.handle` is never called concurrently today ("Application state decision (M3-001)", Concurrency: Flare's multi-worker `serve` needs a `Copyable` handler and `App` is move-only), so the current Flare use never invokes one `App` concurrently. The cap bounds each active JSON parse at about 30 MB; total use is that times the number of parses running at once (one per `App` today, more if a process runs several `App`s or a backend calls `handle` concurrently).
- Why fixed: a setting (per `App` or per route) is new public API with its own lifetime and binding questions, and adding one later is additive; lowering a default later would break clients, so the first value is conservative. 1 MiB sits between Express's 100 kB `json()` default and axum's 2 MB default body limit. Backend limits still apply first (Flare's default `max_body_size` is 10 MB).
- Exactly `MAX_BODY_BYTES` is accepted and one more byte is 413 (`test_body_size_cap`).

**Errors.** Malformed JSON and `from_json` raises are request failures: 400 before the handler, handler not called (`test_malformed_json_is_400_before_the_handler`, 34 texts; `test_field_errors_are_400_and_extra_members_are_ignored`). Handler raises are unchanged: `ToErrorResponse` or the fixed 500. A response-serialization failure (`write_json` raises, NaN, infinity, an unbalanced writer) happens after the handler returned, inside the non-raising `ToResponse.to_response`; `Json` answers it itself with the fixed 500's bytes (`Internal Server Error`, no `Content-Type`), and the handler's `ToErrorResponse` is not called (`test_serialization_failure_is_not_a_handler_error`: a handler declared `raises AppError` whose conversion is 418 still gets 500, and the conversion counter stays 0). It is the same answer as an unconverted handler error but a different path: it is not a handler error, and no partial body is sent.

**Allocation and copies visible to Muntin.** Request: `App.handle` already copies `request.body` into the adapter's arguments. Parsing walks the bytes once and builds one tape (a node list and a child table, no per-value strings; one list of decoded names per object while parsing) in one `_Shared` (two allocations, as for `State`); the spike keeps one more copy of the body in the document, which production can take by move from the adapter argument. `JsonValue` copies add a reference, not a tape copy (`test_json_value_copies_share_the_document`); `string()` decodes and copies out. Measured peak memory over a no-parse baseline: a 4.5 MB array of 50,000 typical records adds 22 MB (about 5x the body, the body copy included), and the worst case, a 5 MB `[0,0,...]` of one-byte values, adds 184 MB (about 37x; the review measured 482 MB with the first spike's string-owning nodes). With the 1 MiB cap above, one parse adds at most about 28 MB (measured). Response: one `String` grown by the writer plus small stacks and one name list per open object, then one header field (two `String`s); the body moves into the `Response`.

**Overload budget.** None added: `get` and `post` keep ten overloads each, and no existing diagnostic changes (the first slice adds a `_Route` field and an adapter step, no signature). Every typed shape takes `Json[T]` where it takes a `FromBody` body or a `ToResponse` result, stateful shapes included (`test_production_app_accepts_json_through_existing_overloads`).

**Candidates** (Mojo 1.1.0 (8189361e), Flare v0.11.0):

| Candidate | Result | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| A. `Json[T]` wrapper, `FromBody`/`ToResponse` through conditional conformance | registers on every existing shape with no App change; documented features only; the Content-Type step is exact because the conversion is Muntin's; DX cost: `Json[...]` in the signature and `.value`/`take()` | **chosen** |
| 2. `FromJson`/`ToJson` application traits | used by A as its requirements, over Muntin-owned `JsonValue`/`JsonWriter` | **chosen, inside A** |
| B. `FromJson` refining `FromBody` (and `ToJson` refining `ToResponse`) with the parent's requirement as a default | compiles and gives DX section 4's exact `def create_user(body: CreateUser) -> User` (`json_known_gaps/refining_trait_default`, must build). The manual documents defaults for a trait's own methods and refinement inheriting requirements, not a refining trait implementing its parent's requirement. Also: the manual's conflicting-defaults rule makes a type that conforms to `ToJson` and another trait defaulting `to_response` an error; the format is bound to the type in every route; a Content-Type step keyed on `FromJson` would fire even where the application overrides `from_body` | rejected (undocumented basis) |
| B′. `FromJson` refining `FromBody`, with the application writing a one-line `from_body` that forwards to an exported parse function (and `ToJson` refining `ToResponse` with a forwarding `to_response`) | documented features only, and DX section 4's bare signature; but two forwarding methods per type, the format bound to the type, the Content-Type step keyed on a conformance whose conversion the application wrote, and the serialization-failure mapping moved into each application `to_response` (non-raising, so each type decides its own failure answer) | rejected (A keeps the conversion and its failure rule Muntin's) |
| C. `Response.json(...)`/helper only | same bytes as A (`test_candidate_c_helper_moves_serialization_failure_into_the_handler`), but no request side; the handler must return `Response` and raise, and a serialization failure becomes a handler error (418 through the handler's `ToErrorResponse` in the test) | rejected (A covers it: `Json(v^).to_response()`) |
| 4a. blanket JSON-capable bodies through an 11th `post` overload (`B: FromJson`) | `ambiguous call to 'post'` for every JSON type, including one conforming to nothing else: the existing `def(var B) -> R` overload bounds `B` by `Movable & Deinitable` and asserts `FromBody` inside (scratch copy) | rejected |
| 4b. blanket through relaxed bounds in the existing overloads (asserts accept `FromJson`; `R: Movable & Deinitable` with `ToResponse`-or-`ToJson` asserted) | compiles on a scratch copy of `app.mojo` and in a retained minimal model (`json_known_gaps/relaxed_result_bound`, must build; a `String` handler still selects the `String` overload, the shorter list) but needs the handler's function type `rebind`-ed to a `downcast` result inside `App`, puts the codec into the adapters, changes 16 bounds/asserts, turns a non-conforming result from `no matching method ... does not conform to trait 'ToResponse'` into an instantiation `constraint failed` (`json_fail/relaxed_result_bound_diagnostic`; 6 pinned production fixtures expect the former), and needs a precedence rule for a type that is both `FromBody` and `FromJson` | rejected |
| 5. no built-in codec | the Muntin-owned codec is small and needs no new dependency, toolchain feature or unsafe code; without it ordinary JSON requires every application to write a parser | rejected |
| Derived codec (reflection defaults) | encoding by `std.reflection` works for a fixed field set (scalars, `List[String]`, `Optional[String]`, nested structs) but not `List[<struct>]` without `__extension` (`json_fail/reflection_list_of_structs`), and an unsupported field fails only at first use, deep in instantiation notes (`json_known_gaps/derived_encode_declared` builds; `json_fail/derived_encode_unsupported_field_at_use`); decoding has no constructor (`reflection_decode_needs_a_constructor`) and needs `Defaultable` dummy initializers (`reflection_decode_requires_defaultable`; this fixture and `derived_encode_unsupported_field_at_use` are illustrative: they pin where a `comptime assert` written in the fixture fires, not compiler wording) or unsafe uninitialized storage | deferred (additive later: a default added to a requirement breaks no conformer) |
| Flare-bundled `json` package | only in the `flare` environment, `__extension`, native library, backend dependency in core | rejected |

**Evidence.**
- `tests/json_spike.mojo` (library side: traits, `Json`, `JsonValue` on `_Shared`, parser, writer, the Content-Type rule, the adapter mirror, C's helper) and `tests/test_spike_json.mojo` (18): production `App` and `TestClient` registering `Json` on `def(B)`, `def(Int, B)`, `def(Int) -> R` and stateful `def(State[S], Int) -> R`; a representative struct with string (escapes, `\u00e9`, a surrogate pair), integer, boolean, float, `List[String]`, an optional member as `null` and absent, a nested object and a list of objects (explicit `write_json` loop), extra members ignored; 34 malformed texts; field errors; the nesting cap; the body cap (at the cap, one byte over, 415/413/400 order, raw `from_body`, non-JSON bodies uncapped); number limits and the float rounding gaps; linear parsing and access; writer escapes and structure (duplicate names included); serialization failure vs handler error; move-only body and result; the explicit override; no default fields on text results; the Content-Type rule (16 values, missing, duplicated); the order on the mirror; document sharing; candidate C.
- `tests/test_spike_json_dx.mojo` (1): DX sections 4 and 5's JSON examples as written there, against the spike.
- `tests/json_lib_only/driver.mojo`: built by `check.sh` without the application module.
- `tests/json_fail` (13) and `tests/json_known_gaps` (3), checked by `check.sh`.
- `compat/flare/json/json_loopback_probe.mojo` (`check_flare.sh`): over loopback through the unchanged adapter, a request `Content-Type` (with `charset`) reaches `Request` unchanged and a missing one stays missing; Flare's `HttpClient.post(url, String)` sets `Content-Type: application/json` itself, so a test meaning "no Content-Type" must build its request; a JSON body with UTF-8 and escapes round-trips and equals `App.handle`, with `Content-Type: application/json` on the wire beside Flare's `Content-Length`; malformed JSON is 400 on both paths; invalid UTF-8 arrives as U+FFFD and parses.
- Scratch copies of `app.mojo` for 4a and 4b (not retained); 4b's mechanism is retained as the minimal model `json_known_gaps/relaxed_result_bound` and its diagnostic fixture. Fresh-context review: two material findings (quadratic and memory-heavy parsing on hostile bodies; silent 1-ulp float errors in Mojo 1.1.0 `atof` and `String(Float64)`) resolved by the linear compact tape and by pinning and documenting the float limits; minors applied (ASCII-only media-type folding, writer duplicate names, B′, internal `JsonValue` initializer, M3-005 statement, illustrative fixtures labeled, 4b model, slice notes). Re-review: both resolved, no new material issue; minors applied (sort-based duplicate names instead of a fixed-seed hash set, the 2^32-byte guard, since replaced by the body cap; this evidence wording). Its third minor, a 23 s test, is TestSuite's figure, which is milliseconds: the compiled suite runs in 0.46 s wall time (measured with timestamps around the binary), the cost-oracle test in about 39 ms. PR review (#32): two material findings, resolved here: the writer's repeated-name check used a hash set with a predictable seed although names may come from request data (now sorted at `end_object`); and the measured memory amplification had no body-limit decision (now the 1 MiB cap with 413). Required fix applied: the M3-009 tests split `App.handle` (successful JSON bodies) from `TestClient` (415 pinned; result-only routes). Minors applied: "strict RFC 8259" restated as the RFC grammar with Muntin's limits; "before the body is read" corrected to before parsing and the handler.
- Mutations (planted, reverted; 30), each red: the body cap off by one, missing from the parser, or its 413 step after `from_body`; the Content-Type rule accepting a missing field, a second field or `+json`, rejecting parameters, or folding Unicode case; the step after `from_body`, before the route value, or applied to every body type; the result without its field; a serialization failure answered 200; the parser accepting trailing commas, duplicate members, leading zeros, lone low surrogates, control bytes, trailing content, or one level past the cap; `int()` accepting fractions; `float()` accepting infinity; the writer accepting non-finite numbers, leaving control bytes unescaped or finishing an open container; an absent member returned as a value; the writer accepting a repeated name; duplicate detection by list scan or element access by sibling walk (the cost oracle).

**Revisit when:**
- the Mojo manual documents a refining trait implementing its parent's requirement (or `json_known_gaps/refining_trait_default` stops building): re-measure B against A;
- `std` gains a JSON module, or a standalone JSON package for the default environment needs only documented features: weigh replacing the codec behind `FromJson`/`ToJson`;
- `std.reflection` gains a constructor, or reflection exposes a field type's parameters (or `__extension` is documented): a derived `from_json`, and a derived `write_json` covering `List[<struct>]`, become possible; a derived `write_json` for the fixed set is possible now, and needs a decision on its late diagnostics;
- `atof` accepts long literals or becomes correctly rounded, or `String(Float64)` prints shortest round-trip text: `test_number_limits_on_mojo_1_1_0` or `test_float_rounding_gaps_on_mojo_1_1_0` flips; drop the documented `float()` limits;
- Muntin gains a bytes body: the codec should reject invalid UTF-8 itself;
- applications need JSON bodies over 1 MiB, or a lower bound: decide a setting (per `App` or per route) for `MAX_BODY_BYTES`;
- `App.handle` becomes concurrent (a `Copyable` `App`, Flare multi-worker, another backend): memory for JSON parsing becomes the per-parse bound times the parses running at once; re-derive the cap;
- clients need `+json` media types or a missing `Content-Type`: an additive relaxation of the request rule;
- `TestClient` gains a way to send header fields (the slice adds none, below: `TestClient.post` reaches a JSON route as 415, an accepted DX cost of requiring `Content-Type`);
- `get`/`post` gain an 11th overload for any reason: re-run the note-budget measurement (this decision adds none).

**Next production slice (M3-009): JSON in production.** Implemented as listed, with the differences recorded in "JSON in production (M3-009)" below.
- `src/muntin/json.mojo` (new): `FromJson`, `ToJson`, `Json[T]` (with `take`), `JsonValue` (compact tape with child table in `_Shared`, keyword-only internal initializer), `JsonWriter` (duplicate names found by sorting at `end_object`), as in the spike; the parser private, linear apart from the per-object name sort, raising on a body over `MAX_BODY_BYTES` (1 MiB, which also keeps the `UInt32` spans exact), indexing the body's bytes and holding the body by move rather than a second copy; private `_JsonBody` and `_json_content_type(headers) -> Bool` (ASCII byte folding). `muntin` exports `Json`, `FromJson`, `ToJson`, `JsonValue` and `JsonWriter`; no `parse_json` export (a raw handler decodes with `Json[T].from_body(req.body)`). Comments in it must avoid the words `check_unsafe.sh` flags outside the storage module (`[Uu]nsafe`, `Allocation`, `OwnedPointer`, ...): say "allocated" or "storage", not "Allocation".
- `src/muntin/app.mojo`: `_Route` gains `json: Bool` (default `False`), set by the eight body overloads (stateless and stateful, `def(B)`/`def(Int, B)`, both result policies) to `conforms_to(B, _JsonBody)`. In `App.handle`, a JSON route appends one more raw argument after the body, `"1"` when `_json_content_type(request.headers)` holds and `""` otherwise; non-JSON body routes get no extra argument, so existing indexing is unchanged. `_call_body`, `_call_int_body`, `_call_state_body` and `_call_state_int_body`, under `comptime if conforms_to(B, _JsonBody)`, convert the route value first, then answer 415 (`Response.text("Unsupported Media Type", status=415)`) when the last argument is empty, then 413 (`Response.text("Content Too Large", status=413)`) when the body argument is longer than `MAX_BODY_BYTES`, then call `from_body`. `_Call`, `_Erased`, the storage module, `check_unsafe.sh`, every overload signature and every existing message are unchanged; `App` stays non-generic.
- Unchanged: `http.mojo`, `body.mojo` (`FromBody` still sees only the body), `state.mojo`, `testing.mojo` (no header-sending method: JSON route tests send `App.handle(Request("POST", target, body, headers^))`, the seam `TestClient` uses; `tests/test_spike_json_dx.mojo` stays on the spike, which has no Content-Type step in production's path), the Flare adapter.
- Tests: `tests/test_json.mojo`, the spike's semantics on production. Successful JSON-body requests go through `App.handle(Request(..., headers^))` with `Content-Type` set, on every typed shape, stateful included: the supported and rejected sets, field errors, 415 and 413 cases and the full order with a query route and an `Int` segment, move-only, a raw handler decoding with `Json[T].from_body` (and its cap). `TestClient.post` on a JSON route is pinned as 415. Routes that only return `Json[T]` (`get`, or `post` with a non-JSON body) go through `TestClient` too: the response field, serialization failure vs handler error, the override, text results with no field. Also the cost oracle (linear parse and index loop), the float pins, and DX sections 4 and 5's examples (through `App.handle` for their bodies). Loopback in `adapters/flare/test_localhost_roundtrip.mojo`: `POST` with `application/json; charset=utf-8` 200 and `Content-Type: application/json` on the wire, missing and `text/plain` 415, a body over 1 MiB 413, malformed 400, each equal to `App.handle`.
- Negative fixtures, `tests/json_api_fail` against production: `Json[T]` without `FromJson` as a body and without `ToJson` as a result, a `FromJson` type as a bare body, moving `body.value` out, a non-raising writer call, a second `JsonValue` mutating the tape.
- Mutations: the spike's on production, plus the verdict appended for every body route (non-JSON indexing shifts; in production this proved inert, see "JSON in production (M3-009)"), the `json` flag missing on a stateful overload, the step missing in one of the four adapters, 415 answered as 400, the 413 step after `from_body` or missing, the cap off by one.
- Diagnostics: every existing must-fail fixture's text identical (no signature changes; to be confirmed in full).
- Docs: DX sections 4 and 5 production status, `docs/SPEC.md`, `feature_list.json`, `AGENT_PROGRESS.md`.
- Not in the slice: a configurable body cap, derived codecs, `+json` or missing `Content-Type`, `TestClient` header methods, `Json(value, status=)`, top-level `List` results, schema/OpenAPI, streaming, typed header extraction, new methods or handler shapes.

### JSON in production (M3-009)

Status: **production**, the M3-008 slice. The contract, the codec's rules, the rejected candidates and the revisit conditions are "JSON codec decision (M3-008)" above; this section records what production does and how it differs from the slice as written.

- `src/muntin/json.mojo` (new): `FromJson`, `ToJson`, `Json[T]` (conditional `FromBody`/`ToResponse`, `take(deinit self)`), `JsonValue` (the compact tape and child table in `_Shared`, keyword-only internal initializer) and `JsonWriter`, as in the spike, with the parser, `_Node`, `_Doc`, `_JsonBody` and `_json_content_type` private. `muntin` exports exactly `Json`, `FromJson`, `ToJson`, `JsonValue` and `JsonWriter`. Differences from the spike:
  - private names: the parser is `_parse_json` (no export; a raw handler decodes with `Json[T].from_body(req.body)`), the limits are `_MAX_BODY_BYTES` (1,048,576) and `_MAX_DEPTH` (64), and the spike's `JsonWriter.finish` is `_finish`: the decided writer API has no `finish`, and `Json.to_response` is its only caller.
  - one copy of the body: the parser takes its copy of the borrowed `from_body` argument and the document takes that copy by move (the spike copied twice). `FromBody.from_body(body: String)` borrows, so this copy remains; removing it would change the public body contract. Measured peak memory over a no-parse baseline (macOS, `/usr/bin/time -l`, the same driver against production and the spike): 1 MiB of `[0,0,...]` adds about 29 MB and parses in 13 ms, 1 MiB of typical records adds about 5 MB in 12 ms, production and spike within measurement noise of each other (the node list's growth dominates the peak, not the body copy).
  - member lookup (`get`, `value[name]`) scans the object's members and decodes escaped names on each comparison, so a `from_json` reading k fields of an m-member object costs O(k·m), bounded by the cap (a review measured about 3 ms per missing-name lookup on a 60k-member object of escaped names); caching decoded names is a possible follow-up, not part of the slice.
  - it imports `_Shared` from the storage module (allowed by `check_unsafe.sh`), `.body` and `.http`; no unsafe operation, no backend import, no `rebind`, reflection or `__extension`.
- `src/muntin/app.mojo`: `_Route` gains `json: Bool` (default `False`); the eight body overloads (stateless and stateful, `def(B)` and `def(Int, B)`, both result policies) pass `json=conforms_to(B, _JsonBody)`. In `App.handle` a JSON route appends `"1"` when `_json_content_type(request.headers)` holds and `""` otherwise, after the body; other routes' arguments are unchanged. `_call_body`, `_call_int_body`, `_call_state_body` and `_call_state_int_body`, under `comptime if conforms_to(B, _JsonBody)`, after the route value: 415 (`_unsupported_media_type()`, `Unsupported Media Type`, no fields) unless the arguments are exactly the route value if any, the body and the verdict `"1"` (`len(args) == 2` and `args[1] == "1"` for a body-only route, `len(args) == 3` and `args[2] == "1"` with a route value; the state never travels in `args`), then 413 (`_content_too_large()`, `Content Too Large`, no fields) when the body argument is longer than `_MAX_BODY_BYTES` bytes, then `from_body`. The slice said "415 when the last argument is empty"; the exact check gives the same answers while the flag is transported, and a body overload that forgot the flag answers 415 on every request: without the verdict the argument count is one short, so a body that reads `"1"` cannot stand in for it (comparing the last argument with `"1"` alone would accept that body; a test sends it, and the mutations below show the arity check is what catches it).
- Unchanged: every overload signature and message (`get` and `post` keep ten overloads each), `_Erased`, `_Call`, `_Bound`, `_handler_storage.mojo`, `check_unsafe.sh` and the unsafe surface, `State` and its marker, `http.mojo`, `body.mojo`, `state.mojo`, `testing.mojo`, `adapters/flare/muntin_flare.mojo`. `App` stays non-generic. The M3-008 spike files are unchanged and still pass: the spike's `Json` conforms to the spike's own `_JsonBody`, so production treats its routes as ordinary `FromBody` routes (`test_body_size_cap`'s "400 without the step" still holds).
- Behavior on production: order on a JSON body route 404 → query value 400 (`App.handle`) → route value 400 → 415 → 413 → JSON 400 (`from_body`: parse or `from_json`) → handler → result conversion, each step measured with every later failure present. `Content-Type`: exactly one field, media type `application/json` compared ASCII case-insensitively with SP/HTAB trimmed, parameters ignored; missing, empty, two fields (equal or not), other types, `+json` types and Unicode look-alikes are 415. Exactly 1 MiB is parsed, one byte more is 413 on every JSON body shape; at the cap a malformed body reaches the parser (400), over it 413 comes first. The parser raises above the cap itself: a raw handler's `Json[T].from_body` raises `JSON body too large`, which its own error model answers (the fixed 500 for a bare `raises`, or the handler's own mapping). A JSON result is 200 with exactly `Content-Type: application/json`; a serialization failure (NaN, infinity, a repeated name, an unbalanced or raising `write_json`) is the fixed 500 without fields and without `ToErrorResponse`, also after a stateful JSON body; a set after `to_response()` wins. `String` results and `Response.text` add no field; application `FromBody` routes need no `Content-Type`, have no cap and keep their 400.
- Diagnostics: the 249 existing must-fail fixtures (every `tests/*_fail` directory), built before and after against production: 119 byte-identical; 130 differ only in `app.mojo` line numbers (the module comment, the two answer helpers and the adapter steps shift the overloads). With line numbers normalized all 249 are identical: error lines, columns, notes and carets (the stack trace that follows `state_fail/closure_handler_storage`'s diagnostic, a pre-existing toolchain crash, excluded). Every expected text matches.
- Evidence:
  - `tests/test_json.mojo` (22): JSON bodies on the ten JSON body routes (the eight shapes, plus the query route value stateless and stateful) through `App.handle` with the field set, each with its result and field count; the representative struct (escaped string, integer, boolean, float, `List[String]`, optional absent/null/present, nested object, list of objects, extra members ignored) and accessor errors; 34 malformed texts on four shapes and duplicate names after escape decoding and per object, 400 before the handler; 12 field errors on four shapes; the nesting cap through a route; the `Content-Type` rule (8 accepted values, 14 refused, missing, empty, duplicated) as a unit and as 415 on every shape; `TestClient.post` pinned as 415 on every shape; the cap at and over 1 MiB on every shape and the 413/400 boundary; the full order on path, query and body-only routes, stateless and stateful; move-only body and result, a borrowed body and `take()`; a raw handler decoding with `Json[T].from_body` (no 415/413 step, cap raising into the fixed 500 or the handler's own 422); `Json[T]` results on `GET`, stateful `GET` and `POST` with a non-JSON body through `TestClient`; serialization failure vs handler error (418 only for the raised `AppError`); the override; no default fields on text results; non-JSON bodies uncapped and unchanged; the number limits and float rounding pins; writer escapes, structure and misuse (11 cases); the cost oracle (an 80k-member object and a 200k-element array read by index, under the cap; the suite runs in under a second of wall time); document sharing.
  - `tests/test_json_dx.mojo` (1): DX sections 4 and 5's JSON examples as written there, against production: JSON bodies through `App.handle`, the 415 cases (missing, `text/plain`, two fields, `application/problem+json`, `TestClient.post`), 413, three 400s, the override through `TestClient`.
  - `tests/json_api_fail` (6, `check.sh`): `Json[T]` without `FromJson` as a body and a bare `FromJson` type as a body (`... its type must conform to FromBody`), `Json[T]` without `ToJson` as a result (`argument type 'Json[In]' does not conform to trait 'ToResponse'`), `body.value^` (`field 'body.value.secret' destroyed out of the middle of a value ...`), a non-raising `write_json` that writes (`cannot call function that may raise ...`), a second `JsonValue` clearing the tape (`invalid use of mutating method on rvalue of type 'List[_Node]'`; it names the internal `_parse_json` and `_doc` only to reach a second handle). The first two prove `Json[T]` is not a `FromBody` without `FromJson`, with the same text as any other non-body type.
  - `adapters/flare/test_localhost_roundtrip.mojo`: `json_app()` serves `POST /greet` (`Json[Greeting]` in and out) and the stateful `POST /greet/{id}`. Over HTTP/1.1, `application/json; charset=utf-8` is 200 with `Content-Type: application/json` on the wire, a missing field (a request built without one) and `text/plain` are 415, a body of 1,048,577 bytes is 413, malformed JSON is 400, and a bad route value with an oversized `text/plain` body is 400; each status, body and `Content-Type` equals `App.handle` with the same fields. The adapter is unchanged.
  - Mutations (planted in scratch copies of `src/muntin`, never in the tree; 49, of which 46 red): the `json` flag missing on each of the eight body overloads; the verdict not transported, or always `"1"`; the arity check dropped together with a missing flag (a body `"1"` then passes as the verdict); the step missing in each of the four adapters; 415 answered as 400; the 413 step missing (stateful `Int` body); 413 before 415; the JSON steps before the route value (stateful); the cap off by one in an adapter and in the parser, and missing from the parser; the `Content-Type` rule accepting a missing field, a second field or `+json`, rejecting parameters, comparing case-sensitively or lowercasing Unicode first; the parser accepting trailing commas, duplicate members, leading zeros, a lone low surrogate, control bytes, trailing content or one level past the cap; duplicate detection by list scan and element access by walking the siblings (the cost oracle); `int()` accepting fractions; `float()` accepting infinity; an absent member returned as a value; the writer accepting a repeated name or non-finite numbers, leaving control bytes unescaped or finishing an open container; a serialization failure answered 200; a JSON result without its field. Green, and observationally inert (no request distinguishes them from production while every body overload sets the flag): the verdict appended for every body route (the slice listed it; the non-JSON adapters read their arguments from the front, so a trailing extra argument changes nothing); the verdict read as "non-empty" instead of `"1"`; the arity check dropped alone. The last two matter only when a flag is missing, which the combined mutation above covers. M3-009's acceptance records inert mutations with their reason instead of forcing them red.

### TestClient request headers decision (M3-010)

Status: **decision** (M3-010; `src/muntin` and `adapters/` are unchanged in it; production since M3-011, "TestClient request headers in production (M3-011)" below). It decides how `muntin.testing.TestClient` sends request header fields, so that a test can reach a `Json[T]` body route through the client instead of `App.handle(Request(..., headers^))`.

**Selected: K, a keyword-only, defaulted `headers` argument on the existing `get` and `post`.**

```mojo
struct TestClient[origin: Origin[mut=False]]:
    def get(self, target: String, *, var headers: Headers = Headers()) -> Response
    def post(self, target: String, body: String, *, var headers: Headers = Headers()) -> Response
```

```mojo
var client = TestClient(app)
client.get("/hello")                          # unchanged
client.post("/users", "name=Ada")             # unchanged

var headers = Headers()
headers.add("Content-Type", "application/json")
client.get("/echo", headers=headers.copy())                 # headers stays usable
client.post("/users", '{"name":"Ada"}', headers=headers^)   # moved
```

- **What the client does:** builds `Request(method, target, body, headers^)` and calls `App.handle`; nothing else. It adds, removes, inspects or merges no field, and it knows nothing of JSON or of the route. A request sent through it is the request `App.handle(Request(...))` receives from the same arguments, so the answers are equal (status, body and response fields).
- **Ownership:** the argument is `var headers: Headers`, moved into the `Request`, as `Request`'s own initializer takes it (M3-002): a caller passes `headers=headers^` or `headers=headers.copy()`; a plain variable is `cannot be implicitly copied`, and using the variable after `^` is `use of uninitialized value`. A copy sent earlier is unaffected by later changes to the original. The default is a new, empty `Headers` on every call.
- **Reading a call:** a request that carries fields says `headers=` at the call, on every method; one that does not, carries none. A positional `Headers` (or a string) after the target, or after the body, is `invalid call to 'get'` / `'post'`: `unexpected argument`. Keyword-only is chosen over a positional last argument because `client.get("/users", h^)` does not say what its second argument is, while `client.get("/users", headers=h^)` does; it is chosen on both methods, rather than on `get` alone, so that one rule holds on every method whether or not it takes a body. It does not mirror `Request`'s argument positions, which the client does not reproduce anyway (it hides the method, and `get` hides the body).
- **Why the surface is minimal, and why K among the equal-cost shapes:** two existing signatures each gain one defaulted parameter. No method, overload, type, field or client state is added; `App`, the registration overloads, `Request`, `Headers`, the JSON code and the Flare adapter are untouched. P and H work as well and have the same surface; K is chosen among them for the readability and the single rule above, not for size or because the measurements force it.
- **Compatibility:** existing calls `client.get(target)` and `client.post(target, body)` keep their spelling and meaning (existing call-site compatibility, measured below). This does not claim compatibility for code that treats the methods as values with their previous exact function signatures. (On Mojo 1.1.0 a bound method value, `var f = client.get`, does not compile on `main` either: `member method closures are not supported`.)

**Rejected (measured on Mojo 1.1.0 in scratch copies of `src/muntin`; production unchanged):**

| Candidate | Measured | Why rejected |
|---|---|---|
| status quo: no client API; JSON body tests call `App.handle(Request(..., headers^))` | what `main` does (`tests/test_json.mojo`, `tests/test_json_dx.mojo`) | a request that needs a field cannot go through the client at all, so the client cannot test a `Json[T]` body route except as 415 |
| P: positional last `var headers: Headers = Headers()` on both | builds; `client.get("/echo", h^)`, `client.post("/echo", "b", h^)` and the `headers=` spelling work; existing callers pass | `client.get("/users", h^)` does not show what its second argument is; one keyword makes every call with fields explicit at the cost of `headers=` |
| H: keyword-only on `get`, positional last on `post` | builds; `client.get("/x", headers=h^)` and `client.post("/x", body, h^)` work, positional on `get` is `unexpected argument`; existing callers pass | the spelling would depend on whether the method takes a body; K gives `get` the same readability with one rule |
| borrowed: `headers: Headers = Headers()`, copied inside | builds; existing callers pass; `client.post("/echo", "b", h)` with a plain variable compiles, and the client copies it | `Request` takes headers by move and rejects a plain variable (`headers_api_fail/request_headers_moved_in.mojo`); the client would accept what `Request` refuses and copy behind the call |
| B: separate overloads `get(target, var headers)` and `post(target, body, var headers)` beside the unchanged ones | builds; identical calls and results to P; existing callers pass | two more methods for exactly P's behavior |
| C1: client-level default fields (`client.headers`, sent on every request) | builds; with `client.headers.add("Content-Type", "application/json")`, `GET /echo` and a plain-text `POST /echo` both receive the field | the fields of a request are not visible at its call; needs a `var client`, per-client state and a merge rule for per-request fields |
| C2: builder (`client.request("POST", target).header(n, v).body(b).send()`) | builds only with `send(deinit self)`; `header` raises; `client.request("PUT", "/users").send()` is 404 | a new public type and four methods; it also opens arbitrary methods on the client, which is outside this item |
| D1: `post_json(target, body)` adding `Content-Type: application/json` | builds; sends the field to the non-JSON route `/echo` too | the client would know JSON; a JSON-only API where a general field argument suffices |
| D2: automatic `Content-Type` when `post`'s body looks like JSON | the same explicit target and body give 200 through the client and 415 through `App.handle(Request(...))` built from them (and over a connection without the field); `/echo` receives a field nobody sent | the client silently changes the `Request` by adding a field the caller did not supply, so its answers differ from every backend's; it hides the M3-008 request rule from the tests meant to exercise it |

**M3-008's revisit condition** "`TestClient` gains a way to send header fields" is the one this decision meets. Its outcome: the request rule is unchanged (a `Json[T]` body needs exactly one `Content-Type` whose media type is `application/json`, otherwise 415), and the accepted cost it recorded ends with the production slice: a test reaches a JSON body route through the client by sending the field. `TestClient.post(target, body)` without it is still 415, so `tests/test_json.mojo`'s pins of that stay true.

**Invariants:**
- `TestClient` builds a Muntin `Request` and calls `App.handle`, nothing else: it adds, removes, inspects or merges no field and never looks at the body or the route. Its answer equals `App.handle(Request(method, target, body, headers))` for the same arguments.
- The bare forms `client.get(target)` and `client.post(target, body)` keep their spelling and meaning: no field is sent.
- The headers argument of `get` and `post` is keyword-only, defaulted to an empty `Headers`, and moved in as `Request`'s initializer moves it; there is no implicit copy and no per-client state. Pinned twice: `tests/testclient_headers_api_fail` pins the client's own spelling and ownership on production `TestClient` (keyword-only, no implicit copy, no use after `^`; `tests/testclient_headers_fail` pinned it on `SpikeClient` until M3-011), and `tests/headers_api_fail/request_headers_moved_in.mojo` pins the premise the client follows, that `Request` takes headers by move.
- No backend type reaches the client; the client stays in `muntin.testing`, outside the backend seam.

**Evidence** (files as of the M3-010 merge; M3-011 deletes the spike and its fixtures):
- `tests/testclient_headers_spike.mojo` (`SpikeClient`: K over production `App`, `Request` and `Headers`; `AutoTypeClient`: D2) and `tests/test_spike_testclient_headers.mojo` (9): the bare `get` and `post` equal production `TestClient` and `App.handle` (200, 404); fields on `GET` and on `POST`, a typed route unaffected by fields; repeated names (`X-A`, `Set-Cookie`, `x-a`, `Set-Cookie`) in order and casing; an empty value present as `""` and distinct from an absent field; `.copy()` leaving the original usable and a later change not reaching a sent request, and `^`; the default empty on every call after calls with fields; a JSON body route 200 with `Content-Type: application/json` and `application/json; charset=utf-8`, 415 without the field (equal to today's `TestClient.post`), with `text/plain` and with two fields, 400 for malformed JSON; every case equal to `App.handle(Request(...))`; D2's parity break.
- `tests/testclient_headers_fail` (4, `check.sh`): a plain variable (`cannot be implicitly copied`), use after `^` (`use of uninitialized value 'h'`), positional headers on `get` and on `post` (`invalid call to 'get': unexpected argument`, `invalid call to 'post': unexpected argument`).
- Existing call-site compatibility: scratch copies of `src/muntin` with K, P, H, the borrowed candidate and B in `testing.mojo` build every `tests/test_*.mojo` from `main` unchanged (273 tests, all pass, as on `main`). With K, every other file that calls `TestClient` also builds unchanged with `--Werror`: `main.mojo`, `tests/json_known_gaps/refining_trait_default.mojo`, and in the `flare` environment `adapters/flare/test_muntin_flare.mojo` (13 pass) and `test_localhost_roundtrip.mojo` (6 pass), beside the other `check_flare.sh` builds. No must-fail fixture calls `TestClient`, so no expected diagnostic can change.
- Mutations of `SpikeClient` (7), each red: fields dropped in `get`, dropped in `post`, `get` sent as `POST`, the body dropped, a field added by `post` (inside a `try`, since `add` raises) (each fails tests); the `*` dropped from `get`, from `post` (each makes its positional fixture build).

**Cost:** one defaulted keyword parameter on each of two methods; a call with fields spells `headers=`. A test that needs fields builds a `Headers` value first, in a raising context (`add` raises); there is no one-line literal for fields.

**Revisit when:**
- `Request`'s initializer changes its ownership of headers (for example, from move to borrow, which `tests/headers_api_fail/request_headers_moved_in.mojo` building would show): revisit whether the client should follow it;
- a fixture in `tests/testclient_headers_api_fail` (in M3-010, `tests/testclient_headers_fail`) builds: the client's spelling or ownership changed;
- `TestClient` gains another request method: revisit whether its header spelling should follow this pattern, keeping it consistent while it stays ergonomic;
- `Headers` gains an inline literal (a Mojo collection literal or a Muntin initializer): consider accepting it without changing the bare forms;
- a test needs the same fields on most requests: a helper in the test, not client state, unless several suites repeat it.

**Next production slice (M3-011): TestClient request headers.**
- `src/muntin/testing.mojo`: `get(self, target: String, *, var headers: Headers = Headers())` sends `Request("GET", target, "", headers^)`; `post(self, target: String, body: String, *, var headers: Headers = Headers())` sends `Request("POST", target, body, headers^)`; `Headers` imported from `.http`; docstrings say the fields are moved in and none are sent by default. Nothing else in `src/muntin` or `adapters/` changes.
- Tests: `tests/test_testclient_headers.mojo`, the spike's semantics on production `TestClient` (bare forms equal to `App.handle`; fields on `GET` and `POST`; repeated names in order and casing; empty value; `.copy()` and `^`; default empty on every call; JSON body route 200 with the field and 415 without, `text/plain` and two fields; malformed 400; each equal to `App.handle(Request(...))`). `tests/test_json_dx.mojo` sends DX section 4's successful JSON bodies through `client.post(target, body, headers=headers^)`, compares one of them with `App.handle(Request(...))`, and keeps its `TestClient(app).post("/users", ok)` 415 pin (no field sent). `tests/test_json.mojo`'s `TestClient.post` 415 pins stay.
- Negative fixtures: `tests/testclient_headers_api_fail` against production `TestClient` (plain variable, use after `^`, positional headers on `get`, positional headers on `post`), registered in `check.sh`.
- Spike retirement: the production tests and fixtures take over, so the slice deletes `tests/testclient_headers_spike.mojo`, `tests/test_spike_testclient_headers.mojo` (`AutoTypeClient` included) and `tests/testclient_headers_fail`, removes the latter from `check.sh`, and points the `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` revisit-index row at `tests/testclient_headers_api_fail` (keeping `tests/headers_api_fail/request_headers_moved_in.mojo`). This record keeps what they measured.
- Mutations (in scratch copies of `src/muntin`), each red: fields dropped in `get`; dropped in `post`; `get` sent as `POST`; the body dropped; a field added by the client; the `*` dropped from `get`; from `post`.
- Diagnostics: every existing expected diagnostic identical (no `App` signature changes).
- Docs: every statement that the client sends no header fields changes to the argument: `docs/DX.md` the status table, "Still targets", section 4's JSON bullet (its example sends the field through the client), and the `TestClient` statements in sections 9, 10 and 19; `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` "Current architecture": "Backend seam" (`TestClient.get(target)` and `.post(target, body)` send no header fields), "JSON" (`TestClient.post` sends no fields) and "Other current limits" (`TestClient` header methods). Also `docs/SPEC.md`, `feature_list.json`, `AGENT_PROGRESS.md`.
- Done: the above; `check.sh`, `test.sh`, `check_flare.sh` and `git diff --check` exit 0; CI `verify` and `flare` pass; a fresh-context review finds no unresolved material issue.
- Not in the slice: JSON-specific client methods or automatic fields, client-level default fields, a builder, new client methods or HTTP methods, typed header extraction, any change to the 415 rule, `Request` or `Headers`.

### TestClient request headers in production (M3-011)

Status: **production**, exactly the M3-010 slice. The decision, rejected candidates, invariants and revisit conditions are "TestClient request headers decision (M3-010)" above.

- `src/muntin/testing.mojo`: `get(self, target: String, *, var headers: Headers = Headers())` sends `Request("GET", target, "", headers^)` and `post(self, target: String, body: String, *, var headers: Headers = Headers())` sends `Request("POST", target, body, headers^)` through `App.handle`; `Headers` is imported from `.http`; the docstrings say the fields are moved in as given and none are sent by default. The methods are the spike's `SpikeClient` unchanged in logic.
- Unchanged: every other file in `src/muntin` (`App`, every overload and message, `Request`, `Headers`, the JSON code, the storage module and the unsafe surface) and `adapters/`. `TestClient` stays in `muntin.testing`, outside the backend seam.
- Behavior: a request sent through the client is the request `App.handle(Request(...))` receives from the same arguments, with the fields in order, casing and repeats, an empty value as a value; the bare forms send none. A JSON body route answers the client 200 with `Content-Type: application/json` and 415 without it, with `text/plain` and with two fields, and 400 for malformed JSON, as through `App.handle`. `tests/test_json.mojo`'s `TestClient.post` 415 pins are unchanged and still pass.
- Diagnostics: the 255 existing must-fail fixtures (every `tests/*_fail` directory except the retired `tests/testclient_headers_fail`), built against `main`'s `src` and this slice's: every log byte-identical once the source path is normalized. No fixture outside the new directory calls `TestClient`.
- Evidence:
  - `tests/test_testclient_headers.mojo` (8, production `TestClient`): the bare `get` and `post` equal to `App.handle` (200, both 404s); fields on `GET` and on `POST`, a typed route unaffected by fields; repeated names (`X-A`, `Set-Cookie`, `x-a`, `Set-Cookie`) in order and casing; an empty value present as `""` and distinct from an absent field; `.copy()` leaving the original usable and a later change not reaching a sent request, and `^`; the default empty on every call after calls with fields; a JSON body route 200 with `Content-Type: application/json` and `application/json; charset=utf-8`, 415 without the field, with `text/plain` and with two fields, 400 for malformed JSON; every case compared with `App.handle(Request(...))` (status, body and response fields in order).
  - `tests/test_json_dx.mojo`: DX section 4's successful JSON bodies through `client.post(target, body, headers=...)`, the first spelled as DX shows it (`headers=headers^`) and compared with `App.handle(Request(...))`; the rejected bodies stay on `App.handle`; the `TestClient(app).post("/users", ok)` 415 pin stays.
  - `tests/testclient_headers_api_fail` (4, `check.sh`), on production `TestClient`, with the spike fixtures' expected texts: a plain variable (`cannot be implicitly copied`), use after `^` (`use of uninitialized value 'h'`), positional headers on `get` and on `post` (`invalid call to 'get': unexpected argument`, `invalid call to 'post': unexpected argument`). Against the previous `TestClient` the first two failed for another reason (`unexpected keyword argument 'headers'`), so they fail for the documented reason only with the slice; the positional pair fails before and after, and the two `*` mutations below are what show they pin keyword-only.
  - Mutations (in scratch copies of `src/muntin`, never in the tree), each red: fields dropped in `get` (4 tests fail); dropped in `post` (4, and `test_json_dx`); `get` sent as `POST` (6, and `test_json_dx`); the body dropped in `post` (4, and `test_json_dx`); a field added by `post` (5); the `*` dropped from `get` and from `post` (each makes its positional fixture build). Beyond the slice's list, a field added by `get` is also red (4).
- Spike retired: `tests/testclient_headers_spike.mojo`, `tests/test_spike_testclient_headers.mojo` (`AutoTypeClient` included) and `tests/testclient_headers_fail` are deleted and `check.sh` registers `tests/testclient_headers_api_fail` in their place; the M3-010 record keeps what they measured. The revisit index points at `tests/testclient_headers_api_fail` and `tests/headers_api_fail/request_headers_moved_in.mojo`.

### Typed header access decision (M3-012)

Status: **decision** (M3-012; `src/muntin` and `adapters/` are unchanged in it; production since M3-013, "Typed header access in production (M3-013)" below). It decides how typed handlers read request header fields, which M3-002 deferred. It settles two costs that earlier records named. First, M3-001 recorded that typed headers would compete for its single injected slot. Second, `get` and `post` already have ten overloads each, which is Mojo 1.1.0's diagnostic note cap (M3-007).

**Selected: C, a Muntin-owned body carrier, `WithHeaders[B]`, in `post`'s existing body slot.**

```mojo
struct WithHeaders[B: FromBody](Deinitable, Movable):
    var headers: Headers              # the request's fields: M3-002's model, unchanged
    var body: B                       # converted by B.from_body, as any body
    def take_body(deinit self) -> B   # moves the body out
```

```mojo
def create_user(
    users: State[Users], input: WithHeaders[Json[CreateUser]]
) raises Unauthorized -> Json[User]:
    var token = input.headers.get("authorization")    # Optional[String]
    if not token or not users[].allows(token.value()):    # a read-only Users method
        raise Unauthorized()                          # ToErrorResponse: 401
    return Json(User(1, input.body.value.name))

app.post["/users"](create_user, users)                # existing stateful overload
app.post["/notes/{id}"](update_note)                  # def(Int, WithHeaders[Note]): existing overload
```

- **How the slot conflict is settled.** M3-012 does not put request headers in the existing injected slot, which stays registration-bound: it holds a `State` bound at the registration call. Header fields are request data, like the body. So they travel with the body, in the body slot, wrapped by a Muntin type the overloads recognize, as `Json[T]` already does there (M3-008). Header access takes no injected slot, adds no injected kind and adds no overload. This decides headers only. Whether request-scoped injection (a middleware context, for example) gets a slot of its own is a separate decision; nothing here rules it out. `State` composes with it unchanged: the stateful body overloads take the carrier as their body (`def(State[S], var WithHeaders[B])`, `def(State[S], Int, var WithHeaders[B])`).
- **Shapes.** The carrier is a body on every shape that takes one: `def(var B)` and `def(Int, var B)`, stateless and stateful, both result policies (the eight body overloads). A handler may borrow it (`input: WithHeaders[B]`) or own it (`var input`). It is not a body on `get`, because `get` has no body slot (below). Raw handlers are unchanged and still receive the whole `Request`.
- **Fields.** `input.headers` is the request's `Headers`, M3-002's model exactly: every field in order, original casing, repeated names as separate fields, empty values as values. `get` returns the first value, matched ASCII case-insensitively, or `None`. `get_all` returns every value in order.
- **Names are runtime values** passed to `get`/`get_all`. The match is ASCII case-insensitive, and an unknown name is `None`. Compile-time names are rejected (below).
- **Missing, repeated and invalid fields: Muntin chooses no status for a header field on a carrier route and gives it no meaning.** A missing field is `None`, and repeated fields are kept. Fields made through the supported `Headers.add`/`set` API, and those the Flare adapter delivers, are valid: `add` validates at insertion, and the adapter answers 400 before `App.handle` to a field Muntin cannot represent (M3-005). The M3-002 known gap (`headers._fields` is reachable by name and bypasses `add`) can build an invalid in-memory `Headers`; on a carrier route that is the fixed 500 (below). Interpreting a value (a scheme, a number, a precondition) belongs to the application. Its failure is a handler error, answered by the declared error type's `ToErrorResponse` or the fixed 500 (M2-013). So there is no pre-handler 400 for header fields. This asymmetry with `from_body`, whose raise is 400, is deliberate. The right status depends on the field: 401 for a missing credential, 412 or 428 for preconditions, 400 for a malformed value. Muntin cannot choose it, and the handler can, through its error type. Two existing checks still answer before the handler on a carrier route: M3-008's `Content-Type` verdict for a `Json[T]` body (415), and the rebuild of a field written through the `_fields` gap (the fixed 500, below). A `FromHeaders` converter with its own 400 would be an additive layer (revisit conditions).
- **The body inside.** `B` must conform to `FromBody` (the struct's bound). It is converted by `B.from_body` exactly as a bare body, and a raise is 400 before the handler. `WithHeaders[Json[T]]` keeps the JSON steps, because the carrier conforms to the private `_JsonBody` exactly when `B` does (conditional conformance). Order on a carrier route: 404 → query value 400 (`App.handle`) → route value 400 → for a JSON body, 415 then 413 → field rebuild 500 (the `_fields` gap only) → `from_body` 400 → handler → result conversion. The carrier is not itself a `FromBody`, so a body alone cannot produce one through body conversion: it has no `from_body`, and nothing can turn a body into an empty-field carrier implicitly. Manual construction stays possible (`WithHeaders(body^, headers^)`), and it takes an explicit `Headers` value. A carrier inside a carrier, `WithHeaders[Int]` and `WithHeaders[Json[T]]` without `T: FromJson` are rejected where the handler is declared.
- **Ownership and cost.** Per request, `App.handle` copies each field's name and value into the route's raw arguments. This is the R1 transport raw routes already use (M3-002), and `_Call`, `_Erased` and the storage module do not change. The adapter rebuilds one `Headers` from those strings and moves it, with the converted body, into the carrier. The carrier is then moved into the handler or lent to it. Cost per request, on carrier routes only: two copies of each field name and value (into the raw arguments, then into the rebuilt `Headers`, whose `add` validates each field again), as raw routes already pay. Every other route's raw arguments are unchanged (measured below). There is no client, route or per-request state, and nothing is copied beyond that transport and rebuild. A field cannot be moved out of the middle of a value on Mojo 1.1.0, so `take_body(deinit self)` moves the body out, as `Json` has `take`.
- **Rebuild failure.** The adapter rebuilds the fields through `Headers.add`, which validates them again. Fields from the supported API and the backend path pass, so the rebuild normally cannot fail. A field written through the `_fields` gap can fail it; the answer is then the fixed 500, as for `_raw_request`, never 400 (`test_invalid_in_memory_fields_are_the_fixed_500`; mutation M14).
- **M2 stays closed.** No M2 signature, binding rule or 400/404/500 boundary changes. Application `FromBody` bodies keep every rule, and the body slot gains a Muntin type besides them. Unlike M3-008's `Json[T]`, which is itself a `FromBody` when `T: FromJson`, the carrier is not one (cost below). The relaxed assert keeps its message, and selection is unchanged (the carrier is not `Int`, `Request` or a `State`).

**`get`: typed `get` handlers get no header access in this decision.** A `get` route that needs header fields uses the raw `get` (`req.headers`), or the stateful raw `get` with its `State`, both existing. Mojo 1.1.0 gives `get` no slot that a Muntin type can ride in for free. Its typed parameters are fixed by type equality (`Int`) or by registration arity (`State[S]`). The ways to add one to `get`'s overload set were measured on scratch copies of `src/muntin` (the parity row is derived from the measured rows, not built):

| Candidate on `get` | Overloads on `get` | Measured |
|---|---|---|
| `def(Headers)`, both result policies, declared last | 12 | 28 of the 259 must-fail fixtures change, all in notes: omitted-note counts rise, and every expected text and every existing candidate note is still printed. A failing call of the new shape itself (`def bad(h: Headers) -> Int`) prints the ten existing candidates and omits both of its own candidates' notes (`(2 more notes omitted.)`), so its misuse is explained by no note about it |
| the same, declared first | 12 | 28 fixtures change, and 11 `tests/state_raw_fail/get_*` fixtures lose their expected text: the stateful raw candidate's note is pushed past the tenth |
| `def(Headers)` and `def(Headers, Int)`, or `def(Headers)` and its stateful twin `def(State[S], Headers)`, both result policies, declared last | 14 | each variant: 28 fixtures change, every expected text kept; omitted-note counts rise further (`storage_fail/two_param_handler`: 4 on `main`, 10 with 12 overloads, 12 with 14) |
| parity with `post`'s four carrier shapes (derived, not built) | 18 | `def(Headers)`, `def(Headers, Int)`, `def(State[S], Headers)` and `def(State[S], Headers, Int)`, two result policies each: the rows above, doubled again |
| relaxed slot: `def(Int)` becomes `def(var V)`, with `V` an `Int` or a carrier | 10 | it works for `GET /me` with a carrier, but changes an M2 public signature (the M2 reopen trigger, "When M2 reopens"). It needs `rebind_var[V]` to pass the parsed `Int` as `V` (`comptime if V == Int` does not refine `V`). 25 fixtures change against the carrier copy, and 6 production fixtures lose their expected text (`body_fail/get_body_handler`, `state_get_fail/missing_state_argument`, `storage_fail/raw_get_string_return`, `request_param_handler`, `string_param_handler`, `two_param_handler`): `no matching method in call to 'get'` becomes `function instantiation failed` with `constraint failed: the handler's parameter is an Int route value or a header carrier`, the diagnostic class M3-008 rejected in 4b. It also does not cover `def(Int, carrier)` |

So every measured typed header shape in `get`'s overload set either drops candidate notes or reopens M2. A separately named registration method for header handlers would have its own overload set, but it is a second spelling for one concept, as candidate G is. Parity with `post` doubles along both the stateless and stateful axes. Typed header access on `get` waits for a decision that restructures registration. More HTTP methods, more route-value arities and broader raw handlers each need that decision too, because each one exceeds the same note budget (`docs/SPEC.md`, "Remaining candidates").

In the meantime, the raw slot already composes. A function parameterized by a typed handler is itself a raw handler, so application code can write `def headers_of[..., f: def(Headers) thin raises E -> R](var req: Request) raises E -> Response` and register `app.get["/me"](headers_of[me])`, or the stateful twin with a `State`, on production `App` today, with no Muntin change (`test_raw_slot_adapter_function_on_production_app`). Muntin does not export such a helper here (candidate G below).

**Overload budget and diagnostics.** No overload is added: `get` and `post` keep ten each. On the scratch copy with the carrier (below), the 259 existing must-fail fixtures were built before and after with source paths and line numbers normalized. 248 are byte-identical. The other 11 differ only in the source line the compiler echoes under the relaxed assert (`comptime assert conforms_to(B, FromBody), (` becomes `... or conforms_to(`). Their error lines, messages, notes and carets are identical, and every expected text matches. The 11 are `compile_fail/post_int_body_without_from_body`, `post_string_body`, `post_without_from_body`, `state_post_typed_int_body_without_from_body`, `state_post_without_from_body`, `typed_post_int_body_without_from_body`, `typed_post_without_from_body`, and `json_api_fail`/`json_fail` `body_without_from_json` and `from_json_alone_is_not_a_body`.

**Candidates** (Mojo 1.1.0 (8189361e)):

| Candidate | Measured | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| C. `WithHeaders[B]` carrier in the body slot | registers on all eight body overloads with one relaxed assert (same message); composes with `Json[T]` (order kept) and with `State`; no overload, no injected kind; on a scratch copy `check.sh`, `test.sh` and `check_flare.sh` exit 0 with every existing test unchanged; diagnostics above. Covers `post` only | **chosen** |
| C′. the carrier conforming to `FromBody` | no assert edit, so all 259 diagnostics would stay byte-identical, and `FromBody` would keep naming every body-slot type; but `WithHeaders[B].from_body(body)` would exist and could only manufacture a carrier with no fields or raise at run time, so a body alone could produce a carrier that silently lacks the request's fields (mutation M11 below: with the conformance, `carrier_has_no_from_body.mojo` builds) | rejected (C makes it a compile error) |
| G+. `get` overloads with a `Headers` parameter | the table above | rejected on Mojo 1.1.0 (note cap; revisit) |
| Grelax. `get`'s `def(Int)` relaxed to a generic slot | the table above | rejected (M2 signature change, `rebind_var`, 6 production texts) |
| H. a `Headers` parameter on every typed shape (no carrier) | `get` +8 and `post` +8 (each shape × with/without headers, twice with `State`), the doubling M3-001 named; the `get` half measured above | rejected |
| I. one generic injected slot (`def(I, Int)` with `I` either `State[S]` or a per-request context `Ctx[S]` holding the state and the fields; the "compose concerns into one context value" form) | `comptime if I == State[S]` does not refine `I`: `value cannot be converted from 'State[S]' to 'I'` (`header_access_fail/generic_slot_does_not_refine.mojo`). With `rebind[I]` the scratch model compiles and runs (`42`, `420`), the 4b class again. A stateless handler has no state to register, so it is not covered. It rewrites the ten stateful signatures. (The model's `Ctx` copies the `State` handle per request; a context borrowing it would not, but would need an origin parameter in a public type. Not measured; the rejection does not rest on it.) | rejected |
| J. compile-time names (`WithHeader[B, "x-api-key"]`, required and single, 400 otherwise) | works on the carrier copy (a missing field is 400). A name is checked only when a function using the type is instantiated, at the registration (`function instantiation failed` / `constraint failed: header name must be an RFC 9110 token`), because `comptime assert` is rejected at struct level (`header_access_fail/struct_level_assert.mojo`). There is one name per carrier type, and a second name cannot nest (the inner type must be a `FromBody`). Muntin would choose 400 for a missing or repeated field, which is wrong for a credential (401) | rejected (runtime names over `Headers`; a typed layer can be added later) |
| G. a Muntin-exported raw-slot adapter (`app.get["/me"](headers_of[me])`) | works on production `App` with no change, stateless and stateful (pinned by the spike test). It is a second spelling for one concept (a registration wrapper on `get`, a signature type on `post`). It has no route values, because raw literals declare none. The helper owns the result policy (one helper per policy and per state) | not exported; recorded as the application-level path for `get` |
| R. raw `Request` only (status quo) | `req.headers` on raw and stateful raw routes, both methods; no typed route value, no typed body conversion and no result policy | kept as the escape hatch, on both methods |
| D0. defer: no typed header API until registration is restructured; header-reading handlers stay raw on both methods | no new public type, no `get`/`post` asymmetry, nothing to migrate if a later request-scoped context covers headers. Cost on `post`, measured in M3-009: a raw handler that decodes a JSON body with `Json[T].from_body` gets no `Content-Type` step (no 415) and no 413 step (the parser raises above the cap, answered by the handler's error model), and it gives up the typed route value and the result policy. So a credential on a JSON `POST` would drop the M3-008 `Content-Type` rule (its CSRF reason) unless each handler re-implemented it | rejected (below) |

**Why C now rather than D0.** The question is whether an asymmetric public type that exists because of Mojo 1.1.0's overload budget is worth adding before registration is restructured. Yes, for three reasons:
- D0 is not neutral on `post`. Deferring means a credential beside a typed JSON body either loses M3-008's `Content-Type` rule and cap (raw) or cannot read the credential (typed). C keeps every typed step and adds only the fields.
- C uses an existing structural asymmetry: `post` has a body slot and `get` does not. The typed-header capability it gives is therefore asymmetric (typed `post` handlers read fields, typed `get` handlers do not) until registration is restructured. C adds nothing to `get` and changes no `get` behavior.
- C adds no overload family, and the carrier can stay a useful input type after a later registration redesign. It does rely on the current body overloads recognizing it (the slice edits the eight body overloads' assert and flag), but it is a body type, not an overload: a later registration redesign, a body-less `POST` or a request-scoped context can be added beside it without changing it. If a context later carries headers for every method, `WithHeaders` is still a valid body type, and retiring it would be a pre-1.0 API removal that the record of that decision would have to justify.
The cost accepted: one public type whose scope is `post` bodies, accepted in the body slot without being a `FromBody` (Cost below), and the revisit condition below if a context generalizes request parts.

**Invariants:**
- The existing injected slot stays registration-bound (`State`); request headers do not use it. Introducing request-scoped injection needs its own decision.
- No `get` or `post` overload is added for header access; both keep ten.
- A carrier route's handler sees the request's fields in order, with their casing, repeats and empty values. On a carrier route Muntin chooses no status for a field and gives it no meaning; the `Content-Type` verdict for a `Json[T]` body (415) and the rebuild of an invalid in-memory field (the fixed 500) still answer before the handler.
- Non-carrier routes' raw arguments are unchanged; typed routes without a carrier receive no header strings.
- The raw `Request` handler stays the low-level escape hatch, on both methods, stateless and stateful.
- No backend type reaches the carrier, and `_Call`, `_Erased` and the storage module do not change.

**Evidence:**
- `tests/header_access_spike.mojo` and `tests/test_spike_header_access.mojo` (10 tests). The model: the carrier and three production-shaped `post` overloads (body only, route value then body, stateful body only), with copies of production's body adapters plus the carrier branch, and production's `_Bound`, JSON verdict and cap. Tests: fields in order, casing and repeats; absent and empty fields left to the handler; a body conversion failure 400 with fields present; the route value first; `State` composition and `take_body`; `Json[T]` inside the carrier with 200, the 415 cases (no `Content-Type`, a field named `1` with value `1` and no `Content-Type`, two `Content-Type` fields), 413 after 415, malformed 400 and a handler-chosen 401 for a missing credential, each in the documented order on both body shapes; plain `Json[T]` and plain body routes receiving no field strings (the exact JSON arity would answer 415); a field written through the `_fields` gap answered with the fixed 500; 404; and the raw-slot adapter function on production `App`, stateless and stateful.
- `tests/header_access_fail` (8, `check.sh`). The spike carrier's surface: no `from_body`, nested, `Int` body, `Json[T]` without `FromJson`, body moved out. Three toolchain premises: `eleven_candidates_drop_a_note.mojo` pins the ten-note cap on a toy with eleven overloads and no detail notes (expected text `cannot be converted from 'Other' to 'UInt16' (1 more notes omitted.)`; the same toy with ten overloads prints no omission marker); `generic_slot_does_not_refine.mojo` (its else branch already uses `rebind[I]`; with `rebind[I]` in the if branch as well it compiles and runs); `struct_level_assert.mojo` (moved into a function, the same assert compiles and fires at instantiation).
- Scratch copy of `src/muntin` with C (not retained): the carrier in a new module and exported; `_Route.headers`; the relaxed assert and the flag in the eight body overloads; the fields appended after the body and the JSON verdict in `App.handle`; the carrier branch in the four body adapters, with the JSON arity widened to an even count after the verdict for every JSON body (the spike and the slice keep the exact arity for non-carrier bodies; the slice confirms mutation M6 red against production). On it, `./scripts/check.sh`, `./scripts/test.sh` (281 existing tests unchanged, plus 5 scratch tests on all four body shapes, stateless and stateful, with `Json[T]` inside) and `./scripts/check_flare.sh` exit 0, with the diagnostics above.
- Scratch copies for G+ (four variants), Grelax and I, and a probe for J (not retained), with the measurements in the tables.
- Mutations of the spike (planted in scratch copies, never in the tree), each red unless marked:
  - fields flag missing on the body-only overload (M1), on the route-value overload (M2) and on the stateful overload (M3);
  - the carrier without its `_JsonBody` forwarding (M4);
  - the JSON flag missing on the body-only overload (M5);
  - fields appended for every body route (M6: the plain `Json[T]` route answers 415);
  - the JSON flag missing with the carrier's parity check removed (M7);
  - the JSON steps before the route value (M8);
  - a body conversion failure answered 500 (M9);
  - the first field read at the verdict's index (M10);
  - for the fixtures: the carrier conforming to `FromBody` makes `carrier_has_no_from_body.mojo` build (M11), and the body bound relaxed to `Movable & Deinitable`, with the check moved into `_from_parts`, makes `nested_carrier`, `carrier_of_int` and `carrier_of_json_without_from_json` build (M12);
  - the rebuild failure answered 400 instead of the fixed 500 (M14);
  - inert: the parity check removed alone (M13). While every JSON carrier route sets its flag, no request tells it apart; it matters only together with a missing flag (M7), as M3-009 recorded for its arity check.

**Cost.** `FromBody` no longer names every type a `post` body slot accepts. `WithHeaders[B]` is a carrier the framework recognizes beside `FromBody` (the overloads and adapters accept `FromBody` or the private `_HeaderCarrier`), so generic application code constrained to `B: FromBody` does not accept it. This is accepted rather than avoided because the alternative, C′, would let a body alone produce a carrier without the request's fields. A handler that reads fields names them in its signature (`WithHeaders[...]`) and reads the body as `input.body` (`input.body.value` for JSON). A missing or malformed field is the application's error type to answer. Typed `get` handlers cannot read fields until a registration-structure decision. Carrier routes copy each field name and value twice per request and validate each field again, as raw routes do.

**Revisit when:**
- `header_access_fail/eleven_candidates_drop_a_note.mojo` loses its expected text (the cap changed): revisit whether `get` takes a header shape directly;
- `header_access_fail/generic_slot_does_not_refine.mojo` compiles (its else branch uses `rebind`, so only the if branch's refinement is pinned): re-measure the generic injected slot (I) and the relaxed `get` slot;
- `header_access_fail/struct_level_assert.mojo` compiles: revisit whether compile-time header names can be checked at the handler's declaration;
- a registration-structure decision is made (more methods, more route-value arities or broader raw handlers need one, since each exceeds the note budget), or M3-001's variadic or closure fixtures compile: revisit whether typed `get` handlers take the carrier through it (made by M3-014, "Registration structure decision (M3-014)": after M3-015, a `Headers` slot or the carrier takes no overload, and the later typed `get` header item chooses between them; chosen by "Typed get header access decision (M3-016)": a `Headers` slot on `get`, last);
- applications repeat the same field parsing with a 400 in many handlers: revisit whether an additive `FromHeaders` converter (with its own pre-handler 400) belongs beside the carrier;
- typed handlers need another request part (cookies, the method, the path) or middleware designs request-scoped context: revisit whether the carrier generalizes before another carrier type is added;
- the R1 transport changes (for example `_Call` carrying the `Request`): revisit the per-request copy;
- applications need to abstract over every type a body slot accepts (generic helpers over `FromBody` that should also take the carrier): revisit whether a public trait should name body-slot types, or the carrier's relation to `FromBody`.

**Next production slice (M3-013): typed header access for `post` handlers.**
- `src/muntin/headers_body.mojo` (new): public `WithHeaders[B: FromBody](Deinitable, Movable, _HeaderCarrier, _JsonBody where conforms_to(B, _JsonBody))` with fields `headers: Headers` and `body: B`, `__init__(out self, var body: B, var headers: Headers)`, `take_body(deinit self) -> B`, and the private `_HeaderCarrier` trait with `@staticmethod _from_parts(body: String, var headers: Headers) raises -> Self` (`B.from_body`, a raise propagating). `muntin` exports `WithHeaders`. Comments in it avoid the words `check_unsafe.sh` flags.
- `src/muntin/app.mojo`:

  - In `App.handle`, a carrier route appends each field's name and value after the body and after the JSON verdict. Other routes' arguments are unchanged.
  - `_call_body`, `_call_int_body`, `_call_state_body` and `_call_state_int_body`: their own `comptime assert conforms_to(B, FromBody)` accepts the carrier too, and the `FromBody` assert moves into the non-carrier branch before `B.from_body` (an `or` assert does not refine `B`). Under `comptime if conforms_to(B, _HeaderCarrier)` they rebuild the fields from the first index after the body (and the verdict, for a JSON body) before conversion (a failure: `_internal_error()`), then build the carrier with `B._from_parts` (a raise: 400). The JSON step keeps its exact arity for non-carrier bodies. For a carrier it requires the verdict `"1"` at its fixed index and an even count after it.

- Unchanged: every overload signature, message and count; `_Erased`, `_Call`, `_Bound`, `_handler_storage.mojo`, `check_unsafe.sh` and the unsafe surface; `State`; `Headers`, `Request` and `Response`; `body.mojo` (`FromBody` still sees only the body); `json.mojo` (the carrier only imports `_JsonBody`); `testing.mojo`; `adapters/flare/muntin_flare.mojo`. `App` stays non-generic.
